**Highlighted author(s):** Derek D. C. Chang  
**Authors:** Derek D. C. Chang, Graeme D. Berk, Mile Gu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25878v1>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2608.25878_figures/2608.25878_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a quantum information-theoretic framework to quantify how much randomness in a quantum process is genuinely unpredictable versus how much is merely structured due to limited memory. By defining the quantum excess entropy, the authors show this quantity equals the quantum mutual information between the past and future halves of the process. This provides a powerful tool to estimate the minimum memory resources needed to simulate complex quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental information-theoretic tools for characterizing the memory content and predictability of open quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding quantum noise and decoherence in physical implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a rigorous quantum measure to distinguish between true, irreducible randomness and structured temporal correlations within a quantum stochastic process.

**Main result.** It establishes that the quantum excess entropy ($E$) quantifies the temporal correlations and provides a lower bound on the memory resources required to model the process using a recurrent quantum circuit.

**Method.** The authors utilize the Choi state representation of process tensors to define and analyze the quantum entropy rate ($s_T$) and the quantum excess entropy ($E$) for stationary quantum processes.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on general stationary quantum stochastic processes, modeled by repeated interactions with a quantum memory system ($M$) via a time-homogeneous quantum operation ($T_{MS}$).

**Key observables.** Quantum entropy rate ($s_T$), Quantum Excess Entropy ($E$), and Quantum Mutual Information (QMI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The process is assumed to be stationary; the memory state $\sigma_M$ and the noise parameter $\lambda$ are key parameters in simulations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process is assumed to be stationary, and the analysis relies on the existence of a time-homogeneous recurrent quantum circuit model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the encoding of multi-time input-output relations via the Choi state and show the convergence of the process entropy growth rate and the alignment of the excess entropy with the past-future QMI.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the quantum entropy rate ($s_T$) as the measure of irreducible randomness, then introduces the excess entropy ($E$) to quantify structured correlations, culminating in proving $E$'s equivalence to the QMI between past and future halves, and bounding $E$ by the memory state entropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For a stochastic process describing observations of a dynamical system, complexity science provides systematic methods to decompose the information produced into true irreducible randomness, and that which corresponds to structure superficially disguised as random but can in fact be learned and predicted. Such methods then equip vital tools for prediction, control and inference of the system's internal structure. However, quantum analogs remain underdeveloped due to the inherent complications of invasive measurements and quantum correlations. By harnessing the Choi state representation of process tensors which encode multi-time input-output relations, we formulate a convergent measure of irreducible randomness in stationary quantum stochastic processes. This directly enables a measure of temporal correlations which lower bounds the memory resources required to replicate the process via a recurrent quantum circuit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general framework to derive exact Hamiltonians for superconducting qudits coupled to complex, realistic linear environments characterized by their impedance or admittance. By extending black-box quantization, it yields divergence-free effective models for both dispersive and dissipative regimes. This establishes a scalable route for designing and analyzing large-scale superconducting quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, systematic, and rigorous theoretical tool to model open quantum systems in superconducting circuits, directly addressing issues like spurious divergences in previous treatments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide simple, exact formulas for constructing Hamiltonians describing superconducting qudits coupled to complex, passive linear environments.

**Main result.** It successfully derives divergence-free dispersive Hamiltonians and transition-resolved weak-coupling master equations for these coupled systems.

**Method.** The method involves characterizing the environment via its impedance or admittance matrices and mapping the environment's port response directly into the Hamiltonian structure using canonical multiport circuit representations.

**Model / system.** The system consists of Josephson-junction-based superconducting qudits coupled to arbitrary linear environments (multiport, multimode, nonreciprocal). The theory is built upon extending black-box quantization to these complex settings.

**Key observables.** Mode structure, frequency renormalizations, environment-mediated interactions, decay rates, and directional cross couplings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak-coupling regime, impedance ($\mathbf{Z}$) or admittance ($\mathbf{Y}$) matrices, and the specific gauge choice (charge or flux gauge).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of effective models is restricted to the two extreme gauges (charge and flux gauges), and the method is best suited for numerical inputs from electromagnetic solvers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) shows constructing exact Hamiltonians from the immittance response for nonreciprocal environments. Figure 2 illustrates coupling nonlinear qudits to linear environments via impedance or admittance.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon prior circuit quantization methods, details the decomposition of the immittance response, derives the exact Hamiltonian in the chosen gauge, and then applies this framework to various physical circuits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Building on the first-order circuit quantization method [arXiv:2304.12252, arXiv:2401.09120], we provide simple formulas to construct exact Hamiltonians for Josephson-junction-based superconducting qudits capacitively, inductively, or galvanically coupled to passive linear environments. These environments may be multiport, multimode, discrete or continuous, reciprocal or nonreciprocal, and are characterized directly by their impedance or admittance matrices. In the weak-coupling regime, we further derive \emph{divergence-free} dispersive Hamiltonians for mode-resolved environments and transition-resolved weak-coupling master equations for dissipative continua. Mode structure, frequency renormalizations, environment-mediated interactions, decay rates, and directional cross couplings then follow from the same causal immittance response, while spurious Lamb-shift divergences arising from uncontrolled approximations in previous treatments are made explicit and avoided. We apply the theory to a set of illustrative circuits comprising a discrete resonator filter, finite-band metamaterial environments, nonreciprocal waveguide-QED systems, and superconducting giant atoms, for which analytical response matrices can be obtained, although the method is particularly well suited to numerical responses from electromagnetic solvers or experimental characterization. We thereby extend the black-box quantization framework to multiport, dissipative, and nonreciprocal settings, establishing a simple and scalable route toward optimized and automated electromagnetic design of large-scale superconducting quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes many-body dynamics in cavity QED systems where interactions are a mix of local and collective. By projecting the dynamics onto specific subspaces, the authors show that the resulting effective interactions are either purely local (if the subspace is product-state-like) or structured non-local (if the subspace is entangled). This framework reveals a new class of dynamics beyond simple local or fully collective models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical understanding of how global constraints (like total angular momentum) can fundamentally alter the nature of local interactions, leading to structured non-local physics relevant for quantum simulation and quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates many-body dynamics governed by a mixture of short-range microscopic interactions and long-range, cavity-mediated collective interactions, where neither locality nor full collectivity is a sufficient description.

**Main result.** The effective dynamics within a subspace is dictated by whether that subspace admits a local product-state basis; entangled subspaces generate structured non-local dynamics, while product-state subspaces retain local spatial structure.

**Method.** The authors analyze the system by projecting the short-range interaction onto subspaces defined by the dominant collective coupling, utilizing techniques involving spherical tensor operators and Wigner-Eckart theorem.

**Model / system.** The model is Cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) applied to interacting spin-1/2 systems, described by a generalized XYZ Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on the effective Hamiltonian derived within specific subspaces (e.g., fixed total spin $S$ or magnetization $M_x$).

**Key observables.** Signal strength $\delta(t)$ used to diagnose non-locality, and the structure of the effective Hamiltonian $H_{	ext{eff}}$ (local vs. non-local terms).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of collective coupling strength to short-range coupling ($ \lambda/J_{ij} 	o \infty $), and the global spin quantum numbers $(S, M_z)$ or $(M_x)^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the clean conceptual limit where cavity-mediated interactions dominate, allowing for spectral separation into distinct subspaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (Rydberg-tweezer cavity), the block-diagonal structure of the effective Hamiltonian distinguishing local and non-local interactions, and the time evolution of signal strength $\delta$ demonstrating non-local signaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical context (Cavity-QED), then analyzing the spectral separation into subspaces, deriving the effective Hamiltonians for different subspaces (e.g., $(M_x)^2=1$ vs. $(S, M_z)$), and finally using non-locality diagnostics (signal strength) to confirm the structured non-local nature of the dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cavity quantum electrodynamics (QED) modifies many-body systems by combining cavity-mediated collective interactions with microscopic short-range interactions. The resulting dynamics lies between the local and fully collective limits, such that neither locality nor collectivity alone provides a complete organizing principle. In the clean conceptual limit of dominant collective coupling, we show that the effective dynamics within each energetically isolated subspace is generically controlled by whether that subspace admits a local product-state basis, and identify exceptions imposed by angular-momentum selection rules. Product-state subspaces generically retain the spatial structure of the microscopic interaction. Entangled subspaces instead generically dress local processes with global operators, generating non-local but highly structured dynamics. We illustrate this by deriving the corresponding effective Hamiltonians in two representative cavity-QED spin models. The cavity-isolated subspace thus becomes a resource for generating competing short-range interactions or globally conditioned local processes, opening a class of many-body dynamics without local or fully collective counterparts.

</details>

### [Dynamical development of long-range spatial coherence in non-equilibrium bosonic condensation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25407v1)

**Summary.** This paper investigates how long-range spatial coherence emerges in exciton-polariton condensates that are driven far from equilibrium. Using time-resolved experiments and simulations, the authors demonstrate that coherence develops in two distinct phases: an initial, oscillatory interaction-driven phase, followed by a stable, high-coherence steady state. This sheds light on the complex, non-equilibrium physics governing bosonic condensation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insight into the non-equilibrium dynamics of coherence formation in cavity QED systems, offering a detailed picture of how coherence builds up beyond simple steady-state assumptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the dynamical development of long-range spatial coherence in non-equilibrium bosonic condensation, focusing on the temporal evolution of coherence as the condensate forms.

**Main result.** Spatial coherence develops through two distinct stages: an early-time, interaction-driven stage featuring transient oscillations, followed by a persistent, spatially-uniform steady-state regime.

**Method.** The research combines time-resolved experiments (using pulsed and continuous wave laser excitation) with numerical modeling to track coherence dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is an exciton-polariton condensate formed in a microcavity, which is a driven-dissipative, non-equilibrium bosonic system.

**Key observables.** Spatial coherence (measured via the first-order correlation function, g^(1)), condensate density, and coherence length (l_coh).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\Delta$), excitation power relative to the threshold ($P/P_{th}$), and the excitation configuration (Ring-Shaped Pulsed Laser vs. Gaussian Continuous Wave).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The early-time dynamics are attributed to underlying microscopic processes of energy relaxation and interactions, distinct from simple density oscillations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setups (RPL and GCW), time-resolved measurements of g^(1) and density, and how coherence length evolves across different detunings and pumping powers.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically explores the coherence dynamics using multiple experimental setups (RPL, GCW) and varying physical parameters (detuning, pump power), confirming the two-stage coherence development across different regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Development of spontaneous coherence is one of the hallmarks of bosonic condensation in a variety of physical systems, such as cold atoms, confined photons, and hybrid light-matter quasiparticles like exciton polaritons in semiconductors. While spatial coherence is well understood once a steady-state condensate has been established, its temporal evolution as the condensate forms is largely unexplored. Here, we explore the dynamical formation of a non-equilibrium, driven-dissipative exciton-polariton condensate through both time-resolved experiments and numerical modeling. Our study reveals that the spatial coherence is established through two distinct stages. The early-time stage is interaction-driven and features transient oscillations in spatial coherence. This stage is followed by a steady-state regime characterized by a spatially-uniform high degree of coherence that extends over the entire size of the system and persists over time. These stages of spatial coherence development occur in both free flowing and confined exciton-polariton systems that undergo a quench - rapid growth of the condensate starting from two different initial settings. Our study offers a deep insight into the process by which long-range spatial coherence is established in a non-equilibrium bosonic condensate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the first observation of cavity magnomechanics in Lithium ferrite (LiFe) by utilizing coherent microwave feedback. The feedback technique is crucial because it suppresses cavity dissipation, dramatically increasing the measurable cooperativity. This allows the researchers to quantify the weak magnomechanical coupling in LiFe, demonstrating a powerful method for probing subtle quantum interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it uses active dissipation engineering (feedback) to access and quantify weak coupling regimes, a core theme in open quantum systems and cavity QED.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge was observing and characterizing the weak magnomechanical coupling in Lithium ferrite (LiFe) when the mechanical signal was otherwise obscured by cavity dissipation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated cavity magnomechanics in LiFe using coherent microwave feedback, significantly increasing the measurable cooperativity and revealing a clear magnomechanically induced transparency feature.

**Method.** The technique involves implementing coherent microwave feedback into the cavity to suppress polariton dissipation, thereby enhancing the detectability of the weak mechanical interaction.

**Model / system.** The system uses a single-crystal LiFe sphere coupled to a microwave cavity, exhibiting cavity magnonics and cavity magnomechanics. The dynamics are analyzed using cavity field equations modified by the feedback loop.

**Key observables.** Polariton linewidth ($	ilde{\kappa}_{\pm}$), Magnomechanical Cooperativity ($C$), and the Magnomechanically Induced Transparency (MMIT) feature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The single-magnon coupling rate ($g_{mb}/2\pi$) for LiFe ($0.26 	ext{ mHz}$) and the enhancement factor provided by the feedback gain ($g_{fb}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that coherent feedback can effectively engineer the cavity dissipation to reveal underlying weak coupling mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the avoided crossing defining polariton branches, the feedback setup, and crucially, show the MMIT feature becoming resolvable as the microwave feedback gain increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system, details the necessity and implementation of coherent microwave feedback, presents quantitative measurements showing the enhancement of cooperativity and the resolution of the MMIT feature, and concludes with a comparison of the coupling strength between LiFe and YIG.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lithium ferrite (LiFe) is a promising material for cavity magnonics because its large spin density enables strong coupling between magnons and microwave photons. Its potential for cavity magnomechanics, however, has remained unexplored. Here, we observe magnomechanical interactions in a single-crystal LiFe sphere using coherent microwave feedback to suppress dissipation of the cavity--magnon polariton. In the absence of sufficient feedback, the narrow mechanical response is difficult to resolve against the much broader polariton background. Increasing the feedback gain reduces the polariton linewidth and correspondingly increases the magnomechanical cooperativity, revealing a clear magnomechanically induced transparency feature. In the measurements presented here, the effective upper-polariton linewidth is reduced from $3.53~\mathrm{MHz}$ without feedback to $5.8~\mathrm{kHz}$ in the presence of feedback, while the measured cooperativity increases from $C=1.9\times10^{-3}$ to $C=0.15$. These measurements provide, to our knowledge, the first observation of cavity magnomechanics in LiFe and demonstrate coherent feedback as a practical route for accessing weak interactions that would otherwise be obscured by dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally investigates how topological order survives and changes in superconducting qubits subjected to decoherence and measurement. By analyzing string order and fidelity correlators, the authors distinguish between different types of symmetry protection, providing a superconducting platform to study the transition from strong to weak spontaneous symmetry breaking in mixed states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, circuit-based realization for probing subtle concepts in topological order and symmetry breaking in open quantum systems, bridging abstract theory with measurable superconducting circuit physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how symmetry-protected topological (SPT) order manifests in mixed quantum states, specifically examining the transition between strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB) and average SPT order.

**Main result.** The experiments demonstrate that underlying SPT correlations can be revealed through distinct signatures—such as robust string order under dephasing or finite nonlinear fidelity correlators—depending on whether the measurement record is conditioned or averaged.

**Method.** The research uses superconducting qubits to prepare and probe a 1D cluster state, employing controlled dephasing, measurement-induced state preparation, and advanced correlator measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a five-qubit superconducting transmon array initialized in a cluster state, governed by a stabilizer Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on symmetry protection ($Z_2 	imes Z_2$) and the effects of open system dynamics (decoherence/measurement).

**Key observables.** Nonlocal string order parameters ($S_o, S'_o, S_e$), conventional long-range correlators, nonlinear fidelity correlators ($F_{SWSSB}$), and cluster-state fidelity ($F_{LB}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dephasing strengths ($\pi_i$), measurement bases ($	heta_i$), and the ratio distinguishing strong vs. average symmetry protection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that measurement-induced phase accumulation can be mitigated using rotationally invariant combinations of correlators, and that readout errors can be corrected via Bayesian unfolding.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, the pulse sequence for cluster state preparation, the decay of string order parameters under dephasing, and the measurement of various correlators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first characterizing the ideal SPT order via string order, then introducing controlled dephasing to observe the transition to ASPT order, and finally analyzing measurement-conditioned states to reveal signatures of SW-SSB.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally investigate how symmetry-protected topological order in a one-dimensional cluster state is transformed by measurement and decoherence in a five-qubit superconducting array. We first characterize the state's nonlocal string order and show that controlled dephasing selectively suppresses one symmetry sector while leaving the other robust, consistent with average symmetry-protected topological order. We then measure one sublattice in a tunable basis and show that the remaining qubits are driven between a long-range-entangled GHZ state and a paramagnetic state. When the measurement record is discarded, the conventional long-range correlator vanishes while a nonlinear fidelity correlator remains finite, providing a finite-size signature of strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking. These experiments demonstrate how conditioning, averaging, and decoherence reveal distinct manifestations of order encoded in the same underlying cluster state, and establish a superconducting-circuit setting for probing mixed-state symmetry and topology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows how to simulate the Yang-Lee critical point, a non-Hermitian physical phenomenon, using the controlled dissipation of an open quantum system. By engineering the Lindbladian evolution, the system's dynamics effectively mimic the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian, allowing for direct measurement of critical correlation functions without relying on post-selection. This establishes a general method for simulating arbitrary non-Hermitian physics on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, physically realizable framework for simulating non-Hermitian quantum dynamics, which is crucial for understanding phenomena like exceptional points and non-unitary statistical mechanics, directly applicable to quantum simulation hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of realizing the Yang-Lee critical point, which is governed by a non-Hermitian theory, as a physical process in an open quantum system without resorting to post-selection techniques.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the Yang-Lee theory can be effectively described by the non-Hermitian generator of the open system's Lindbladian evolution, allowing for the direct measurement of critical correlation functions.

**Method.** The approach involves mapping the classical Yang-Lee model to the time evolution of an open quantum system governed by a Lindbladian superoperator, which is then shown to be equivalent to non-Hermitian Hamiltonian dynamics in a doubled Hilbert space.

**Model / system.** The system is an open quantum system of qubits evolving under a Lindbladian. This dynamics is engineered to mimic the non-Hermitian time evolution associated with the Yang-Lee transfer matrix, which describes the critical point of the Ising model in an imaginary field.

**Key observables.** Spin correlation functions, Loschmidt Echo correlator, and the time-dependence of linear observables transitioning from underdamped to overdamped decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunable parameters ($	heta, \Delta, \gamma$) controlling the competition between unitary and dissipative dynamics, and the dimensionless field $h = 	heta/\Delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the existence of an extensive number of weak-symmetries of the Lindbladian, and the analysis often involves approximations in the continuous-time limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of the mapping from the Yang-Lee partition function to the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian. Figure S1 illustrates the quantum circuit realizing the nearest-neighbor Kraus operator.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mapping from the classical Yang-Lee model to the open quantum system dynamics. It then details the mathematical machinery, including the use of the doubled Hilbert space and the Lindbladian evolution. Finally, it analyzes specific observables and demonstrates the connection to the critical point and general non-Hermitian embedding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the $d+0$-dimensional Yang-Lee theory describing classical Ising spins in an imaginary magnetic field can be realized, without post-selection, within a $(d-1)+1$ open quantum system whose dynamics consist of local unitaries and engineered dissipation. Competition between the coherent unitary and dissipative dynamics drives a transition wherein the time-dependence of a particular class of linear observables changes from damped oscillatory "underdamped" to purely exponential "overdamped" decay. Our construction relies on an extensive number of weak-symmetries of the Lindbladian fixed by the choice of observable but is otherwise exact. Consequently, we show that the dynamics are described by the non-Hermitian generator of the Yang-Lee transfer matrix, leading to an effective Yang-Lee theory defined on the spacetime history of the open system. By locally modifying the dynamics, we directly measure spin correlation functions of the Yang-Lee theory as well as a related "Loschmidt Echo" correlator which we detail. We explicitly show that the required dissipation channels can be obtained through local $2$-qubit gates and discuss potential realization on near-term quantum simulator devices. Finally, we generalize our construction to embed arbitrary non-Hermitian Hamiltonians within an open quantum system under Lindbladian time-evolution without post-selection, drawing connections between unconditional open quantum dynamics, exceptional point physics, and non-unitary statistical mechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how tuning the mass anisotropy in polariton condensates controls their pattern formation and stability. By analyzing the system's linear excitations, the authors show that unequal effective masses induce stable, directionally preferred stripe patterns. This establishes mass anisotropy as a powerful control knob for directional instability in these non-equilibrium quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium many-body dynamics in an optical cavity setting, providing a clear mechanism (mass anisotropy) to control pattern selection and directional instabilities, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how mass anisotropy affects modulational instability, pattern formation, and collective dynamics in driven-dissipative polariton condensates.

**Main result.** Mass anisotropy is identified as a key control parameter, inducing stripe patterns along the direction of the greater effective mass, which is a directional effect absent in isotropic systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs linear stability analysis using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes approximation on the open-dissipative Gross-Pitaevskii equation.

**Model / system.** The system is an exciton-polariton condensate, modeled as a driven-dissipative system with an effective mass that can be tuned to exhibit anisotropy.

**Key observables.** Stripe pattern formation, center-of-mass oscillations, most unstable wavelength ($\lambda_m$), and the imaginary part of the Bogoliubov spectrum ($	ext{Im } \omega_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mass anisotropy ratio ($m_y/m_x$), pump power ($P$), and pump width ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a small perturbation approximation around the steady state and uses a mean-field description.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Bogoliubov spectrum showing instability regions, and time evolution plots demonstrate the formation of anisotropic stripe patterns and oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the governing equations and performing linear stability analysis to determine instability criteria, followed by numerical simulations to observe pattern formation and directional dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by recent experimental advances toward electrical control of the effective mass and the realization of anisotropic mass distributions, we investigate the impact of mass anisotropy on driven-dissipative polariton condensates, focusing on modulational instability, pattern formation, and the collective dynamics of the emergent patterns. We show that unequal effective masses along the two orthogonal directions induce stripe pattern formation in an exciton-polariton condensate pumped by a non-resonant Gaussian laser beam above a critical mass ratio. A Bogoliubov analysis of the homogeneously pumped system reveals that mass anisotropy leaves the modulational-instability criteria unchanged but reshapes the unstable-mode dispersion by reducing the most unstable wave- length along the direction of larger effective mass with increasing mass ratio. This introduces an anisotropy-controlled length scale for pattern selection. When the pump width becomes comparable to the most unstable wavelength, the condensate becomes modulationally unstable and develops stripe patterns along the direction of larger effective mass, accompanied by particle-number fluctua- tions and center-of-mass oscillations along the same direction. As the pump width increases further, the stripes become distorted and develop spatial irregularities during the time evolution, indicative of modulational instability. Nevertheless, the anisotropic mass distribution preserves a pronounced directional preference, in contrast to the fully disordered patterns observed in the isotropic-mass case. Our results identify mass anisotropy as a key control parameter for directional modulational instability and pattern formation in nonequilibrium polariton condensates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel method to generate multiple, distinct quantum skyrmion topologies simultaneously from a single metasurface device. By coupling high-dimensional photonic entanglement (OAM-OAM pairs) to a structured metasurface, the authors overcome the limitation of single-topology generation. This establishes a new, compact pathway for engineering complex quantum states for quantum information applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the generation and control of complex topological quantum states using structured light and engineered matter, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and photonic quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing methods for generating quantum skyrmions are limited to producing pre-determined two-dimensional qubit states with a single topology.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated the compact generation of multiple, distinct topological quantum states from a single metasurface device by interfacing it with high-dimensional photonic entanglement.

**Method.** The approach uses a metasurface to induce a non-local, probabilistic topological transformation on a high-dimensional OAM-OAM entangled photonic state.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes quantum skyrmions realized in optical platforms, specifically employing biphoton entanglement generated via SPDC and interacting with a metasurface (J-plate) designed with amorphous silicon nanopillars.

**Key observables.** Topological winding number (N), Concurrence (C), Purity ($\gamma$), and Fidelity (F) are used to characterize the generated states and their entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The topological control is achieved by varying parameters $(m, n)$ related to the metasurface transformation, yielding multiple distinct topological classes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ability to characterize the joint state correlation structure and the assumption that the metasurface interaction can be modeled to induce controlled topological shifts.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the proposed scheme (biphoton HD entanglement $ightarrow$ multiple topologies) against single-photon methods, and show the metasurface design and resulting shifted OAM spiral bandwidth.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitation of single-topology generation to proposing the metasurface-entanglement interface, detailing the physical implementation using OAM-OAM entangled pairs, and finally quantifying the resulting multi-topological states using various quantum metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum skyrmions are topological structures that have garnered significant interest due to their demonstrated robustness and versatility across diverse optical platforms. However, existing approaches for their generation are limited to producing pre-determined two dimensional qubit states with a single topology. Here we create multi-dimensional topological states by introducing a non-local interaction between high-dimensional photonic entanglement and a metasurface, where the topological transformation induced by the metasurface is made non-deterministic by the probabilistic nature of the interfacing entangled state. Within this framework, we demonstrate that individual quantum states can host multiple co-existing topologies that are only revealed upon measurement, allowing for their parallel transport within distinct spatial mode channels. We confirm this by revealing the rich topological landscape within our modified Hilbert space while controlling the desired output topology by orbital angular momentum (OAM) projections on one of the entangled photons, producing multiple non-local polarization-OAM entangled states characterized by distinct topological classes, all from a single metasurface device. Our results reveal new capability when structured high-dimensional entanglement is interfaced with structured matter capable of coupling photonic degrees of freedom, establishing a new pathway for the compact generation of complex quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theory for minimizing energy dissipation when extracting work from non-equilibrium systems using a finite sequence of controlled quenches. By relating the optimal protocols to the Fisher-Rao geodesic, the authors establish quantitative lower bounds on the maximum extractable work. The framework is successfully applied to model complex physical processes, including optical trapping and enzyme kinetics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, quantitative bounds on non-equilibrium work extraction, connecting fundamental thermodynamic concepts (dissipation) to geometric measures (Fisher-Rao distance) in a discrete, step-wise manner.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the optimal sequence of intermediary Hamiltonians for work extraction from non-equilibrium systems when restricted to a finite number of quench-relax steps, aiming to minimize dissipated work.

**Main result.** The minimal dissipated work scales asymptotically as the square of the Fisher-Rao distance divided by $2eta N$, and a lower bound on extracted work is derived based on this distance.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as minimizing a sum of Kullback-Leibler divergences over a sequence of quenches, leading to optimal intermediaries defined by a recurrence relation involving the Lambert W function.

**Model / system.** The analysis covers general non-equilibrium systems, extending to specific models like optical traps (1D Gaussian distributions) and enzyme-catalyzed reactions (3-state Markov chains).

**Key observables.** Dissipated work ($W_{	ext{diss}}$), extracted work ($W_{	ext{ext}}$), and the Fisher-Rao distance ($d_{	ext{FR}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of steps ($N$), inverse temperature ($eta$), and the coupling/rate parameters ($\delta, \epsilon$) in the applied models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the restriction to a finite number ($N$) of quench-relax steps, and in applications, assumptions about timescales (e.g., separation of timescales in catalysis) are made.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optimal intermediary distributions (sometimes bimodal) compared to geodesics, show the scaling of mean dissipated work with $N$, and schematically depict the enzyme-catalyzed reaction process.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general theory of finite relaxation protocols minimizing KL divergence sums, deriving the optimal intermediary form using the Lambert W function, and then applying this framework to concrete examples like optical traps and enzyme catalysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Work extraction from nonequilibrium systems is a major challenge across biological, chemical, physical, and engineering systems. Idealized protocols generally require a quasistatic relaxation stage in which the Hamiltonian is gradually adjusted through a continuum of intermediaries. Here, we consider protocols restricted to a finite number $N$ of intermediary Hamiltonians, consisting of a sequence of quench-relax steps. We determine the sequence of quenches that minimizes the dissipated work, which can be expressed in terms of a recurrence involving the Lambert function. The optimal sequence converges to the Fisher-Rao geodesic, saturating known leading-order dissipation bounds at large $N$. We obtain lower bounds on work extraction from a nonequilibrium distribution as a function of its Fisher-Rao distance to equilibrium. We extend and apply the framework in two simple models: (i) an optical trap experiment, showing that the optimal intermediary distribution can be bimodal even for unimodal initial and final distributions, and (ii) an enzyme-catalyzed reaction, showing that that accounting for relaxation time in addition to dissipation can favor barrier-lowering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates quantum Motility-Induced Phase Separation (MIPS) in a model of active hard-core bosons. By incorporating nearest-neighbor repulsive interactions, the authors show that the system can achieve genuine phase separation while simultaneously maintaining long-distance quantum coherence. This establishes quantum MIPS as a novel, non-equilibrium quantum state driven by the interplay between activity and quantum mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the intersection of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (active matter) and quantum physics, providing a theoretical framework for understanding how quantum coherence can stabilize novel, non-classical steady states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the existence and nature of quantum Motility-Induced Phase Separation (MIPS), specifically addressing how quantum coherence interacts with dissipative self-propulsion in active matter.

**Main result.** The authors provide evidence that quantum MIPS can occur in a model of active hard-core bosons, establishing it as a genuinely non-equilibrium quantum state distinct from classical MIPS.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical modeling using a Lindblad master equation and numerical simulations, including the Monte Carlo Wave-Function (MCWF) method, to track quantum dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional chain of hard-core bosons with an internal spin degree of freedom, evolving under a combination of coherent Hamiltonian dynamics (tumble) and dissipative jumps (directed hopping/run).

**Key observables.** Superlinear number fluctuations ($\Delta N^2_{	ext{sub}} \propto N^2_{	ext{sub}}$), characteristic length scale ($L$) derived from the structure factor, quantum coherence ($C_d$), and spatial entanglement (Negativity).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of coupling strengths ($g/\hbar\gamma$) and the strength of nearest-neighbor repulsive interactions ($I$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the MCWF method and assumes that the breakdown of factorization in the extended model is necessary to stabilize macroscopic phase separation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate superlinear number fluctuations, the growth of the characteristic length scale $L$ upon adding repulsion, and the convergence of quantum coherence and spin negativity in the steady state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing a minimal model showing microphase separation, then extending the model with nearest-neighbor repulsion to achieve genuine phase separation, and finally confirming the quantum nature by demonstrating persistent long-distance quantum coherence and entanglement in the clustered steady states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motility-induced phase separation (MIPS) describes a central clustering phenomenon in active matter systems where particles spontaneously separate into dense and dilute phases even in the absence of interparticle attractive forces. Recent theoretical and experimental efforts have taken the first steps to extend active matter concepts to the quantum level. However, whether a genuine quantum analog of MIPS exists and how quantum coherence would compete or cooperate with the dissipative self-propulsion has remained open so far. Here, we provide evidence that quantum MIPS can occur in a model of active hard-core bosons in one dimension. Our model yields superlinear number fluctuations characteristic of MIPS, leading to microphase separation with large but finite cluster size. Adding nearest-neighbor repulsive interactions, we find numerical evidence for restoring genuine phase separation with a divergent correlation length. Crucially, the clustered steady states maintain a long-distance quantum coherence, revealing a genuinely quantum feature with no classical counterpart. These results provide a foundation for exploring quantum MIPS and suggest that the coherence-activity interplay can generate new types of nonequilibrium quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the geometric phase as a universal diagnostic tool for understanding steady-state reorganization in driven-dissipative quantum oscillators. By analyzing the phase winding protocol, the authors show that the GP captures critical phenomena like phase locking and dissipative phase transitions. This method is highly valuable because it probes the entire eigensystem structure, offering a more comprehensive view than traditional observables.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, unified geometric tool to diagnose complex non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems, moving beyond simple mean-field observables to probe the underlying structure of the steady-state eigensystem.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to find a unified geometric diagnostic to characterize steady-state reorganization in driven-dissipative quantum oscillators, linking phase locking and dissipative phase transitions.

**Main result.** The geometric phase (GP) provides a unified, experimentally accessible diagnostic that captures the reorganization of the steady-state eigensystem across different critical regimes.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes the kinematic geometric phase approach, particularly by winding the drive phase to generate a closed loop of non-equilibrium steady states, reducing the GP to an eigensystem functional.

**Model / system.** The study models driven-dissipative bosonic systems, including the Quantum Stuart-Landau (QSL) oscillator and the two-photon-driven Kerr resonator, governed by a master equation.

**Key observables.** Geometric Phase ($\Gamma_g$), Phase Coherence ($S_q$), and the local geometric response ($\chi_{\Gamma_g}$), which is decomposed into eigenvalue ($\chi_\lambda$) and eigenvector ($\chi_n$) contributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive strengths ($E, \eta$), damping rates ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2, \kappa$), and the proximity to critical points like the Hopf threshold.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions in the weak-drive limit, and for measurement, it employs mixed-state interferometry (Ramsey sequence) to bypass full density matrix reconstruction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the perturbative GP response showing Arnold-tongue behavior, the GP near the Hopf threshold showing nonperturbative reorganization, and the decomposition of the GP response across first and second-order transitions in the Kerr resonator.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general geometric phase for nonunitary evolution, applying it to the QSL oscillator to show its connection to phase locking, and finally analyzing the Kerr resonator to decompose the response into eigenvalue and eigenvector contributions near phase transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven-dissipative quantum oscillators lock their phase, deform their limit cycles, and undergo dissipative phase transitions, yet these behaviors are read from unrelated quantities defined on the same steady-state density matrix. We show that a single geometric quantity organizes them. Winding the phase of the drive generates a closed loop of nonequilibrium steady states, and because the Liouvillian is covariant under number rotations, the kinematic mixed-state geometric phase of this loop reduces exactly to an eigensystem functional of a single steady state. Under weak driving, it is governed by the same nearest-neighbor coherences that produce phase locking and inherits the Arnold tongue of synchronization. Near the Hopf threshold, it registers the nonperturbative reorganization of the steady-state eigenvectors. And in the squeezing-driven Kerr resonator, it develops distinct signatures at the first and second order dissipative phase transitions. The geometric phase thus provides a unified and experimentally accessible characterization of steady-state reorganization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the successful demonstration of a heterogeneously integrated coupled-cavity modulator using thin-film lithium niobate on a silicon platform. The device achieves tunable frequency-mode transformations, such as 50/50 beam splitting, essential for building integrated quantum photonic circuits. This work showcases a scalable, foundry-compatible approach for quantum information processing hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, integrated platform for quantum light manipulation, directly addressing hardware bottlenecks in scalable quantum communication and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for efficient components to coherently mix frequency modes for frequency-encoded photonic qubits, which is crucial for scalable high-dimensional quantum information processing.

**Main result.** They demonstrated a heterogeneously integrated coupled-cavity modulator capable of tunable bidirectional frequency-mode transformations, including 50/50 beam splitting and near-complete frequency swapping.

**Method.** The experiment involves fabricating and characterizing a coupled-cavity modulator by bonding thin-film lithium niobate onto a silicon photonics platform and driving it with both optical and microwave signals.

**Model / system.** The system is a coupled-cavity modulator built on a silicon photonics platform using thin-film lithium niobate. It functions by coupling hybridized optical supermodes using a microwave drive, allowing for controlled frequency transformations.

**Key observables.** Transmission spectra, power extinction ratios (e.g., >20 dB pump extinction), and the realization of specific beam-splitting ratios (e.g., 50/50).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Supermode splitting (10 GHz), coupling rates ($\kappa_e, \kappa_i$), and the electro-optic modulation strength ($V_\pi L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes compatibility with foundry-fabricated silicon photonics platforms and the ability to bond high-quality electro-optic films like TFLN.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the heterogeneous Si/TFLN platform fabrication process flow, and subsequent figures show characterization results like avoided mode crossings and frequency conversion spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical system design, presents the fabrication process flow, and then reports experimental characterization results showing the device's ability to perform frequency beam splitting and shifting.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Frequency encoded photonic qubits promise a scalable path towards high-dimensional quantum information processing, but require efficient components for coherently mixing frequency modes. Coupled cavity modulators provide this functionality by using only a single driving microwave tone to couple hybridized optical supermodes. Here, we demonstrate a heterogeneously integrated thin-film lithium-niobate-on-silicon coupled-cavity modulator that realizes tunable bidirectional frequency mode transformations, including \(50/50\) beam splitting and near complete frequency swapping with \(>20~\mathrm{dB}\) pump extinction at a \(10~\mathrm{GHz}\) supermode splitting. Because the electro-optic film is bonded onto a foundry fabricated silicon photonics platform, the approach is compatible with co-integration of photon pair sources, spectral filters, active tuning elements, and single photon detectors. We also bond thin-film lithium tantalate onto the same coupled-cavity platform, demonstrating material flexibility for scalable integrated frequency bin quantum photonic circuits.

</details>

**Authors:** Thiparat Chotibut, Ewa Gudowska-Nowak, Maciej A. Nowak  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25030v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work determines the entropy production rate (EPR) in non-equilibrium systems governed by network interactions. It shows that the EPR is fundamentally controlled by the statistics of directed walks on the interaction graph. Crucially, it proves that this rate converges to a universal square-root law, suggesting that the mechanism generating dissipation is dictated by shared directed-walk properties rather than specific network details.

**Why it may be interesting.** The universal square-root law derived from purely topological/combinatorial walk properties provides a powerful, model-independent constraint on the thermodynamics of complex, non-equilibrium systems, which is relevant for understanding dissipation in quantum open systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how the complex structure of an interaction network generates the entropy production rate (EPR), a key measure of irreversibility in non-equilibrium steady states.

**Main result.** The mean EPR per node universally follows a square-root law, $\phi^*(g) = 1 - \sqrt{1-g^2}$, regardless of whether the network is recurrent or deep acyclic.

**Method.** The analysis involves recasting the EPR as a quadratic form in walk non-reciprocity matrices, leading to a walk-counting formulation that is then analyzed in the large-N limit.

**Model / system.** The model uses multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck dynamics on a network described by an interaction matrix $W$. The system is analyzed by examining directed walks on this network structure.

**Key observables.** Entropy Production Rate ($\Phi_{total}$), walk non-reciprocity matrices ($A(r)$), and walk quantities (shared-endpoint pairs $K_{r,\ell}$ and closed walks $L_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength parameter $g \in [0,1)$, network depth, and the spectral radius $ho(W)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the contractivity condition $ho(W) < 1$ for power series expansions, and the derivation of the universal law requires satisfying a 'matched-walk moment condition' in the large-N limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the universal square-root curve across different network ensembles (recurrent vs. acyclic), confirming convergence to the analytic prediction $\phi^*(g)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the EPR in terms of walk non-reciprocity (Theorem 1), reformulates it using walk counting (Theorem 2), connects this to spectral properties (Theorem 3), and finally derives the universal square-root law by analyzing the limit behavior under specific moment conditions (Theorem 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The entropy production rate (EPR) quantifies irreversibility of a nonequilibrium steady state, yet standard formulas obscure how a complex interaction network generates it. For multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck dynamics on such networks, we express the EPR as a quadratic form in antisymmetric matrices measuring the nonreciprocity of aggregate directed walks at every length, and, equivalently, as two weighted-walk quantities: pairs of directed walks sharing both endpoints, and directed closed walks. For diagonalizable interactions, an exact correspondence translates these walk quantities into eigenvalues and biorthogonal eigenvector overlaps. Across dense, sparse, and deep acyclic random interactions satisfying matched-walk conditions, the mean EPR per node universally follows the square-root law $φ_*(g)=1-\sqrt{1-g^2}$, where $g \in [0,1)$ parametrizes the interaction strength. Deep acyclic interaction matrices are nilpotent, with all eigenvalues fixed at zero for every $g$, yet, as their depth increases, their mean EPR per node approaches $φ_*(g)$. Thus, the square-root law arises from directed walk properties, rather than from a shared spectral density or specific network topology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on an optimized, fast, and low-leakage measurement protocol for superconducting transmon qubits. By combining a large resonator decay rate with optimal dispersive coupling, the authors achieved sub-100 ns readout times while keeping measurement-induced leakage rates extremely low. This advances the feasibility of building large-scale quantum processors requiring high-fidelity state readout.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental benchmarks for optimizing superconducting qubit readout, directly addressing the fidelity and speed requirements necessary for scaling up quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is achieving fast, accurate, and low-leakage qubit measurement, which is a critical bottleneck for implementing quantum error correction protocols.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a measurement process with a total duration of 97 ns and a very low per-measurement leakage rate of $2.7 	imes 10^{-5}$, enabling high-fidelity readout.

**Method.** The measurement fidelity was characterized using a combination of pulse sequences, state-dependent measurements, and theoretical analysis (Floquet simulations) to quantify leakage and timing.

**Model / system.** The system involves a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to a readout resonator, operating in the small qubit-resonator detuning regime.

**Key observables.** Total measurement duration (97 ns), assignment error (0.17%), per-measurement leakage rate ($2.7 	imes 10^{-5}$), and state-dependent effective resonator decay rates ($\kappa_{0,	ext{eff}}/2\pi = 37.5$ MHz, $\kappa_{1,	ext{eff}}/2\pi = 24.1$ MHz).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large effective resonator decay rate ($\kappa_{	ext{eff}}/2\pi = 30.8$ MHz) and a dispersive shift near the optimal SNR-per-photon condition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that multiphoton resonances are weakly coupled and traversed diabatically, and that the readout process can be accurately modeled by input-output theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the coupled modes for readout (with/without a filter), the pulse sequence for characterization, and the time-domain response showing the decay of the ac Stark shift and photon number.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first characterizing the readout parameters using pulse sequences, then determining the total measurement duration by monitoring the decay of the measurement-induced error, and finally analyzing leakage mechanisms using Floquet simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast, accurate, and low-leakage qubit measurement is a key requirement for quantum error correction. Here, we demonstrate measurement of a superconducting transmon qubit with a total duration of 97(1) ns, defined as the time from the start of the measurement pulse until the measurement-induced error on a subsequent $π$-pulse operation falls below $10^{-4}$. By combining a large state-averaged resonator decay rate of $κ_\mathrm{eff}/2π$ = 30.8 MHz with a dispersive shift close to the optimal SNR-per-photon condition, we achieve an assignment error of 0.17(1)% using a 58-ns measurement pulse, with residual readout photons depleting passively in tens of nanoseconds without an active depletion pulse. Using a repeated-measurement sequence together with a leakage-sensitive measurement, we benchmark the measurement-induced state transitions, finding a per-measurement leakage rate of $2.7(2) \times 10^{-5}$, only twice the background rate and two orders of magnitude below the measurement-induced relaxation rate, which dominates the assignment error. Floquet simulations indicate that the multiphoton resonances present at the operating point are weakly coupled and traversed diabatically, without causing leakage. These results demonstrate that a large resonator decay rate, combined with a dispersive shift close to the optimal SNR-per-photon condition, can enable fast, high-fidelity, low-leakage dispersive readout at small qubit-resonator detuning.

</details>

<sub>FIG. 4. Two-photon gate calibration and randomized benchmarking. a, Readout amplitude as a function of the drive amplitude Agf, showing Rabi oscillations of the |g⟩- |f⟩ transition. Circles are data and the solid line is a fit. The right axis marks the readout values corresponding to |g⟩and |f⟩. The inset shows the process underlying the two-photon |g⟩- |f⟩drive (see text). b, Pulse sequence for randomized benchmarking measurement used in c. c, Average gate fidelity as a function of ggain (bottom axis) and κgain (top axis).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces and experimentally realizes a noise-biased qubit in a transmon circuit by applying a single microwave tone to engineer a frequency-selective gain channel. This technique suppresses single-photon loss errors, leading to significant enhancements in qubit coherence times. Furthermore, the authors demonstrate that this gain-engineered qubit can serve as a robust ancilla for implementing advanced quantum error correction protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physical implementation challenges of fault-tolerant quantum computing by providing a scalable, hardware-efficient method to suppress dominant error channels in superconducting qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to engineer noise bias in superconducting qubits to reduce hardware overhead for fault-tolerant quantum computation while maintaining high operational fidelity.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an order-of-magnitude enhancement in relaxation time by encoding the qubit in the $|gangle-|fangle$ states using engineered gain, while maintaining compatibility with fast, high-fidelity gate operations.

**Method.** The technique involves introducing a frequency-selective gain channel via a single microwave tone to counteract single-photon loss errors, thereby biasing the noise towards a manageable error type.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a standard transmon-readout resonator circuit. The mechanism is modeled by adding an engineered gain interaction Hamiltonian, which is activated via a four-wave mixing process.

**Key observables.** Relaxation times ($T_{f1}, T_{g1}$), echo-coherence time ($T_{2E}$), single-qubit gate fidelity ($F$), and Holevo variance ($\Delta V_{x,p}/V_{0,x,p}$) for error detection.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Engineered gain rate ($\kappa_{gain}$), coupling strength ($g_{gain}$), and comparison to conventional $|gangle-|eangle$ encoding.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The gain acts selectively on the $|eangle - |fangle$ transition due to the transmon's large anharmonicity, and the analysis often relies on adiabatic elimination limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept diagram, resonance mapping, time evolution of readout signals, and comparisons of decay times and gate fidelities as a function of engineered gain strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of noise bias and experimentally realizes it in a transmon circuit. It then quantifies the improvement in coherence times and gate fidelity compared to standard encodings. Finally, it extends the concept to use the gain-engineered qubit as an ancilla for implementing and testing various quantum error correction codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interaction between a qubit and its environment can be engineered such that one error channel dominates over all others, resulting in noise bias. This property enables error correction codes to focus on the dominant error type, thereby significantly reducing the number of physical systems required for fault-tolerant quantum computation. However, engineering noise bias typically introduces complexity at the physical system level, which decreases its usefulness by limiting scalability. Here, we introduce and experimentally realize a noise-biased qubit in a standard transmon-readout resonator circuit, one of the most common superconducting architectures, by only adding a single microwave tone. We encode the qubit in the transmon $|\mathrm{g}\rangle$- and $|\mathrm{f}\rangle$-states, and engineer a frequency-selective gain channel that counteracts single-photon loss errors between the computational states. We demonstrate an order-of-magnitude enhancement in relaxation time compared to the $|\mathrm{g}\rangle-|\mathrm{e}\rangle$ encoding, conceding only a factor-of-two decrease in the echo-coherence time. Furthermore, we show that this qubit is compatible with fast, high-fidelity operations. Our results open a path towards using this system as a simple building-block for hardware-efficient quantum error detection and correction schemes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive diagnostic framework to accurately assess quantum hardware performance by moving beyond aggregate scores. It tests superconducting and trapped-ion systems using structured protocols like Round-Trip State Echo (RTSE) across various workloads. The key finding is that hardware capability is highly dependent on the specific task, compilation, and execution context, necessitating workload-resolved diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, structured methodology for benchmarking quantum hardware that moves beyond simple volume metrics, offering insights into how specific communication and error-correction protocols perform under realistic, time-stamped execution constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Determining the true, workload-dependent performance of quantum hardware, as aggregate metrics fail to capture task-specific limitations.

**Main result.** Hardware performance must be assessed using diagnostics tied to the specific workload, compilation, and execution snapshot, rather than relying on single, universal scores.

**Method.** The authors developed a structured 'screen-and-stress' profile, utilizing protocols like Round-Trip State Echo (RTSE) and deletion-recovery cells, to test hardware under controlled, task-specific conditions.

**Model / system.** The study tests superconducting qubits (IQM Emerald) and trapped-ion systems (IonQ Forte) using fixed input ensembles and structured circuit families.

**Key observables.** Round-Trip State Echo (RTSE) average, selected-output return probability, and route-level contrasts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Workload type, compilation details, execution snapshot timing, and route length ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are explicitly framed as 'execution-workload diagnostics' and do not claim to measure fundamental error suppression or coding gain.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit flow for five workload families (including RTSE, teleportation, etc.) and detail the compiled resource counts for these structured tests.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of aggregate metrics, details the structured diagnostic profile (RTSE, etc.), presents comparative results across different hardware platforms and workloads, and concludes by emphasizing the workload-indexed nature of the findings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

End-to-end quantum-hardware scores need not transfer across workloads, compilations, or execution times. We specify a compilation-explicit screen-and-stress profile whose opening diagnostic is round-trip state echo (RTSE): prepare one of four tetrahedral qubit states at a route root, move it out and back by swaps, apply inverse preparation at the root, and record zero. An execution snapshot means a dated submitted task batch together with its captured capability document where available, not a certified calibration epoch. On sparse superconducting hardware, a byte-identical communication rerun changed route-level contrasts although the aggregate RTSE estimates differed by only 0.00125. In a separate prospectively frozen two-window length study, RTSE and the remote-inverse do-nothing predecessor's root marginal both fell from length 2 to length 10; the prespecified interaction did not support superior RTSE retention. The mean selected-output return probability across 64 deletion-recovery cells changed from 0.738 to 0.624 between IQM execution snapshots. On a trapped-ion service advertising all-to-all connectivity among five submitted virtual wires, recovery was 0.911 and 0.923 in two windows, exceeding the frozen two-thirds reference; recovery-minus-adjoint-control differences were 0.446 and 0.443. These are execution-workload diagnostics, not coding-gain, error-suppression, physical-loss, fault-tolerance, or architecture-ranking claims. The results support assessment indexed by workload, placement or virtual-wire contract, compilation, architecture, and execution snapshot.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the practical challenge of simulating the 2D Hubbard model on real quantum hardware by comparing multiple fermion-to-qubit mappings. The authors demonstrate that the Bonsai mapping is superior for heavy-hexagon processors, and they improve the simulation by using simulated annealing to optimize the resulting qubit Hamiltonian. This confirms the viability of performing large-scale, hardware-aware quantum simulations for strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the critical gap between theoretical quantum simulation models and the practical constraints of physical quantum hardware, providing actionable guidelines for mapping complex fermionic physics onto superconducting qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving efficient quantum simulation of strongly correlated fermionic systems, like the 2D Hubbard model, on current near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The Bonsai mapping was identified as the most hardware-efficient baseline transformation for heavy-hexagon processors, and combining it with simulated annealing significantly reduced the cost function for large-scale 2D simulations.

**Method.** The authors benchmarked three fermion-to-qubit mappings (JW, BK, Bonsai) by analyzing Pauli weight and SWAP overhead on specific hardware architectures, followed by optimizing the Hamiltonian construction using simulated annealing.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the two-dimensional (2D) spinful Fermi-Hubbard model, simulated on superconducting quantum processors featuring heavy-hexagon connectivity. The simulation involves mapping fermionic operators to Pauli strings via unitary embeddings.

**Key observables.** Pauli weight, SWAP overhead, local magnetization, energy, and spatial spin–spin correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Heavy-hexagon chip size (up to $6 	imes 6$ lattices), coupling constants ($J, U$), and noise parameters ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes linear fermion-to-qubit mappings ($n=m$) and relies on the hardware connectivity graph to assess overheads.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the theoretical structures of the mappings (JW, BK, Bonsai) and show the time evolution of local magnetization on $4 	imes 4$ lattices, confirming the preservation of the initial Néel order despite noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first analyzing the theoretical efficiency of various fermion-to-qubit mappings on specific hardware topologies. It then applies the best mapping (Bonsai) to construct and optimize the Hamiltonian for the 2D Hubbard model, culminating in time evolution simulations and noise mitigation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum simulation of strongly correlated fermionic systems is among the most promising near- term applications of quantum computing, but its practical efficiency depends critically on the choice of fermion-to-qubit mapping and on the connectivity of the underlying hardware. In this work we address this problem in the context of the two-dimensional Hubbard model, simulated on IBM superconducting quantum processors with heavy-hexagon connectivity. We numerically benchmark the Jordan-Wigner, Bravyi-Kitaev, and Bonsai transformations, evaluating their Pauli weight and SWAP overhead across seven heavy-hexagon chips of increasing size. We show that, while the Bravyi-Kitaev mapping initially exhibits a lower Pauli weight, this advantage is eliminated once routing costs are taken into account, confirming the Bonsai mapping as the most hardware-efficient baseline transformation for this architecture. We then use the Bonsai mapping to construct the qubit Hamiltonian of the 2D spinful Fermi-Hubbard model, introducing a simulated annealing algorithm that optimizes the assignment of Majorana strings to lattice sites, reducing the cost function by nearly 50% percent. Finally, we simulate on quantum hardware the time evolution of fermionic states up to 6x6 lattices, confirming the viability of the Bonsai encoding for hardware-aware large-scale two-dimensional simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the realization of a high-connectivity (connectivity-four) quantum processor using fluxonium qubits coupled via resonators. The authors achieved state-of-the-art gate fidelities and demonstrated robust multi-qubit entanglement preparation. This establishes the fluxonium-resonator-fluxonium architecture as a viable, scalable path toward error-correction-compatible quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses scalable hardware implementation for quantum computation, providing concrete metrics (high fidelity, controlled connectivity) for superconducting qubit platforms relevant to quantum error correction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is extending qubit connectivity to two-dimensional lattices for quantum error correction in fluxonium processors while suppressing parasitic interactions.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a connectivity-four unit cell using a fluxonium-resonator-fluxonium architecture, achieving high-fidelity single-qubit gates (>99.9%) and controlled two-qubit gates (>99%).

**Method.** Gate fidelities were measured using randomized benchmarking (RB/IRB), and multi-qubit entanglement was verified by preparing and measuring GHZ states via quantum state tomography.

**Model / system.** The system uses fluxonium qubits coupled via lumped-element resonator couplers, realizing a connectivity-four unit cell. The Hamiltonian includes terms for qubits and the resonator, with coupling mediated by state-dependent dispersive shifts.

**Key observables.** Single-qubit gate fidelities, two-qubit gate fidelities, GHZ state tomographic fidelity, and residual static ZZ interactions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Single-qubit fidelity > 99.9%; Two-qubit fidelity > 99%; Residual ZZ interaction < 1 kHz; Connectivity = 4.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coupling strategy relies on exploiting selection rules at the lower sweet-spot where even-numbered transitions are forbidden.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the chip architecture, showing the FRF coupling scheme and the spectral diagram of dispersive shifts. Other figures detail fidelity measurements and error mitigation protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the connectivity-four architecture, details the Hamiltonian and coupling mechanism, presents experimental results for single-qubit and two-qubit gates, and concludes by validating multi-qubit entanglement preparation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central challenge in fluxonium-based quantum processors is the extension of the qubit connectivity to two-dimensional lattices compatible with quantum code-error correction. Here, we present a fluxonium quantum processor that employs lumped-element resonator couplers which realizes, for the first time, a connectivity-four unit cell with suppressed parasitic interactions. We achieve parallel single-qubit gate fidelities exceeding 99.9 % in simultaneous randomized benchmarking experiments, while maintaining residual static ZZ interactions below 1 kHz across all coupled qubit pairs. We implement resonator-induced phase (RIP) gates and benchmark two-qubit gate fidelities exceeding 99 % using interleaved randomized benchmarking. To cancel spectator errors observed in two-qubit operations, we implement a refocused RIP gate, recovering coherent control in the presence of multi-qubit connectivity. Furthermore, we prepare Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger states of up to five qubits with a tomographic fidelity of 90 %, verifying multi-qubit entanglement within the unit cell. These results establish the fluxonium-resonator-fluxonium architecture as a viable approach to realizing densely connected fluxonium processors and provide a scalable path toward quantum error-correction-compatible processor architectures.

</details>

### [The View from Within: What Can Embedded Observers (Not) Learn?](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25800v1)

**Authors:** Tomáš Gonda, Johannes Fankhauser, Gemma De les Coves

The inaccessible subspaces are Iret = P and Ipre = Q....</sub>
