**Summary.** This work reports experimental signatures of a ferro-Josephson effect in twisted graphene heterostructures. The effect arises from spin-polarized currents causing domain wall precession, which modulates the Berry curvature. The evidence is presented through sharp, field-sensitive peaks in resistance measurements, establishing the material as a sensitive probe of isospin domain-wall dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study connects topological phenomena (Berry curvature, Josephson effect) to emergent magnetism and transport in 2D materials, providing a platform to study non-linear, current-driven quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reports on the observation of a ferro-Josephson effect in a twisted graphene van der Waals heterostructure.

**Main result.** Signatures consistent with the ferro-Josephson effect are found, characterized by a sharp peak in longitudinal resistance at zero in-plane magnetic field ($B_{\parallel}=0$) and resonant features in differential resistance.

**Method.** Electrical transport measurements ($R_{xx}, R_{xy}, dV/dI$) are performed as a function of applied magnetic fields ($B_{\perp}, B_{\parallel}$), gate voltage, and current bias.

**Model / system.** The system is a twisted graphene van der Waals heterostructure, where spin and valley textures are coupled by exchange, Hund's coupling, and spin-orbit interactions. The effect is theoretically linked to spin-transfer torque driving domain wall precession.

**Key observables.** Sharp peak in $R_{xx}$ at $B_{\parallel}=0$; sharp resonances in $dV/dI$; anomalous Hall effect ($R_{xy}$ hysteresis); negative in-plane magnetoresistance ($\Delta R_{xx}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($T \approx 25 	ext{ mK}$); small in-plane magnetic fields ($	ext{microtesla}$ to $	ext{millitesla}$); current bias ($I_{ac}$ dependence).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed features are interpreted as evidence for the current-driven precession of spin-domain-wall moments, which is distinct from simple depinning or Joule heating.

**Figures summary.** Figures map out phase diagrams ($R_{xx}$ vs. gate voltage), show $R_{xy}$ hysteresis, and provide detailed $R_{xx}(B_{\parallel})$ and $dV/dI$ spectra demonstrating the field and current dependence of the ferro-Josephson signature.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements across various magnetic field and gate voltage regimes, first establishing the anomalous Hall effect and then focusing on the current-dependent, field-sensitive features in the differential resistance to confirm the ferro-Josephson mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When a spin-polarized current is driven across a magnetic domain wall, the resulting spin-transfer torque could, beyond a critical threshold, set the wall's moments into precession. This precession would modulate the Berry curvature experienced by electrons traversing the wall, producing an electromotive force that is topological in nature, proportional to the precession frequency, mapping precisely onto the DC Josephson effect and leading to the name ferro-Josephson effect. We report signatures consistent with this effect in a twisted graphene van der Waals heterostructure, where spin and valley textures are linked by exchange, Hund's coupling, and spin-orbit interactions. Tuned to fillings where the isospin degeneracy is spontaneously broken, the samples develop a sharp peak in the longitudinal resistance within a fraction of a millitesla of $B_\parallel=0$---a peak that disappears as the current is reduced toward zero. In differential resistance the feature resolves into sharp resonances that disperse with $B_\parallel$ on microtesla and picoampere scales. We argue that these arise from the current-driven precession of spin-domain-wall moments, in competition with the in-plane anisotropy set by a minuscule applied field, and that they establish nonlinear transport as a sensitive probe of isospin domain-wall dynamics at energy scales far below $k_BT$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally and numerically investigates the spin fluctuations in the 1D Kondo metal CeCo2Ga8. By combining inelastic neutron scattering and ARPES with DMRG simulations, the authors confirm the material's quasi-1D nature and place it within the incommensurate phase of the 1D Kondo lattice diagram. This validates the use of the Kondo lattice model as a microscopic description for heavy fermion behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The successful mapping of complex heavy fermion physics onto a solvable 1D quantum model (Kondo lattice) provides a powerful benchmark for understanding emergent quantum phases in strongly correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the spin fluctuations and electronic structure in the 1D Kondo metal CeCo2Ga8 and determine if its behavior can be accurately modeled by the 1D Kondo lattice model.

**Main result.** The material exhibits highly one-dimensional electronic and magnetic behavior, showing incommensurate spin fluctuations that are successfully reproduced by non-perturbative DMRG simulations of the 1D Kondo lattice model.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements using inelastic neutron spectroscopy and ARPES with theoretical interpretation via Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the heavy fermion metal CeCo2Ga8, which is modeled using the 1D Kondo lattice model. This model describes the competition between Kondo screening and magnetic interactions.

**Key observables.** Incommensurate magnetic fluctuations ($\ell=0.354(2)$), quasi-1D electronic band structure (ARPES), and the localized spin susceptibility ($\chi_{SS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-energy scale ($\approx 1 	ext{ meV}$), Kondo coupling strengths ($J_H, J_k$), and the dimensionality (highly 1D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the Kondo lattice model is the appropriate minimal description for the heavy fermion physics, despite potential neglect of higher-dimensional bands.

**Figures summary.** Figures show neutron scattering intensity mapping out incommensurate peaks, ARPES contours confirming 1D dispersion, and DMRG results illustrating the spectral evolution across different theoretical phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first presenting experimental evidence (neutron scattering and ARPES) confirming 1D, incommensurate fluctuations, followed by theoretical modeling using DMRG to map these features onto the known phase diagram of the 1D Kondo lattice.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an experimental and numerical study of the spin fluctuations in 1D Kondo metal CeCo$_2$Ga$_8$. Using inelastic neutron spectroscopy, we measure highly one-dimensional magnetism with low-energy incommensurate short-ranged magnetic fluctuations. ARPES similarly shows a highly one-dimensional electronic band structure, confirming the one-dimensional nature of the system. We use density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) simulations of the 1D Kondo lattice model to interpret the measured spectrum, which successfully reproduce the neutron scattering features. We are thus able to place CeCo$_2$Ga$_8$ within the emergent incommensurate phase of the 1D Kondo lattice phase diagram, and demonstrate that the Kondo lattice simulated non-perturbatively is an accurate microscopic model for heavy fermion physics. This shows CeCo$_2$Ga$_8$ to be one-dimensional despite its complexities, and reveals a coexistence of low-energy Kondo and magnetic features in its inelastic spectrum.

</details>

**Summary.** This work studies how magnetic monopoles affect the electronic spectrum of fermions in a 3D U(1) gauge theory on a lattice. The key finding is that monopoles induce anomalously small eigenvalues in the fermion operator, which are directly related to the monopole density. This suggests a topological mechanism causing flavor symmetry breaking in the system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of fermion zero modes coupled to topological defects (monopoles) is a fundamental topic in condensed matter and high-energy physics, relevant to understanding topological superconductivity or fractionalization in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the anomalous behavior of Wilson fermions when coupled to magnetic monopoles within a three-dimensional compact U(1) gauge theory.

**Main result.** The presence of monopoles leads to anomalously small, exponentially decaying eigenvalues in the fermion spectrum, with the number of these eigenvalues corresponding to the monopole density, indicating flavor symmetry breaking.

**Method.** The analysis uses lattice gauge theory techniques, studying the low-lying spectrum of the Wilson-Dirac operator and relating its behavior to the monopole density.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-dimensional compact U(1) gauge theory regularized on a lattice, probed by two flavors of two-component Wilson fermions.

**Key observables.** Low-lying eigenvalues of the Wilson-Dirac operator, monopole density ($ho_M$), and the fermion condensate ($\Sigma_f$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice gauge coupling ($eta$), bare Wilson mass ($m_w$), and lattice size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the monopole density can be calculated independently and that the small eigenvalues are localized at monopole/anti-monopole sites.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of monopole density and action density on $eta$, and show the exponential decay of low-lying eigenvalues as a function of lattice size $L$ and Wilson mass $m_w$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the existence and density of monopoles in the U(1) theory. It then analyzes the fermion spectrum, demonstrating that the presence of monopoles causes specific eigenvalues to approach zero, correlating this behavior with the monopole count.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Three dimensional compact U(1) gauge theory supports a finite density of monopoles at a fixed lattice gauge coupling. After showing evidence for the expected dependence on the lattice coupling of the density of monopoles, we study the low lying spectrum of a two flavors of two-component Wilson fermions as a function of the bare Wilson mass. The Wilson-Dirac operator that realizes the two flavors are hermitian conjugate of each other to restore parity invariance and the commutativity of the two operators expected in the typical continuum limit is not the case in the presence of monopoles for all values of the lattice gauge coupling. This results in an anomalous behavior where we see clear evidence for the presence of very small eigenvalues for a wide range of a particular sign of the bare Wilson mass. These eigenmodes are typically in one-to-one correspondence with the number of monopoles as defined by the gauge field and the spectrum associated with these modes are separated by a gap from the bulk in the asymptotic regime of the lattice gauge coupling. The eigenvalues come in pairs around zero and the eigenvalues of one sign couple to the monopoles while the modes of the opposite sign couple to the anti-monopole implying flavor symmetry breaking.

</details>

**Summary.** This work calibrates the phase offset for quantum oscillations measured via the ac magnetostrictive coefficient in the topological antiferromagnet $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$. By directly comparing the oscillation phases from magnetization and magnetostriction, the authors confirm a $\pi/2$ phase shift predicted by thermodynamics. This calibration is crucial for accurately extracting Berry phase information from magnetostrictive measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the rigorous phase comparison technique is a powerful diagnostic tool for probing fundamental symmetries and topological properties of electronic band structures, which has parallels in understanding quantum phases in other strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to experimentally calibrate the phase offset associated with quantum oscillations when measured using the ac magnetostrictive coefficient.

**Main result.** The study observed a relative phase shift of approximately $\pi/2$ between the quantum oscillations in magnetization and the ac magnetostrictive coefficient, consistent with the Maxwell relation.

**Method.** The authors measured quantum oscillations in both magnetization and the ac magnetostrictive coefficient on a single crystal of $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ and compared the resulting phases.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the topological antiferromagnet $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$. Its electronic structure is analyzed using DFT calculations, revealing Dirac-like bands near the Fermi level, which are sensitive to the magnetic ordering.

**Key observables.** Oscillation frequencies (around 160 T), relative phase shift ($\approx \pi/2$), and the temperature dependence of oscillation amplitudes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field up to 14 T, fundamental frequency $F \approx 160$ T, and the relative phase shift $\Delta\varphi \approx \pi/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the validity of the Maxwell relation linking the magnetostrictive coefficient to the stress derivative of magnetization, and the use of the leading-phase approximation for theoretical derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the composite magnetoelectric setup, the crystal structure, and the calculated electronic band structure/Fermi surface of $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using the Lifshitz-Kosevich theory and Maxwell relations, details the experimental setup using the composite ME method, presents comparative measurements of oscillations in M and $\epsilon$, and concludes by using the phase comparison to calibrate the required phase offset.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Berry phase accumulated along a cyclotron orbit encodes important information about electronic band topology and is commonly inferred from the phase of quantum oscillations. Measurements of the ac magnetostrictive coefficient have recently emerged as a sensitive thermodynamic probe of quantum oscillations, but the phase offset has not been experimentally calibrated. Here, using the topological antiferromagnet YbMnBi$_2$, we calibrate this offset by directly comparing quantum oscillations in magnetization with those in the ac magnetostrictive coefficient. Measurements of both responses on the same single crystal reveal a single fundamental frequency of approximately 160 T in fields up to 14 T, enabling a direct phase comparison free from ambiguities associated with multiple frequencies. We observe an approximately $π/2$ relative phase shift between the two oscillatory responses, consistent with the Maxwell relation linking the magnetostrictive coefficient to the stress derivative of magnetization. Our results establish the appropriate phase needed to extract cyclotron-orbit phase information from quantum oscillations in the ac magnetostrictive coefficient.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.26061v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper determines the fundamental limits for purifying unknown unitary quantum channels corrupted by noise. It establishes that the optimal strategy achieves a query complexity scaling of $\Theta(d^2 p / \epsilon)$, which is an improvement over prior methods. The work rigorously proves these bounds using advanced mathematical tools from quantum information theory and representation theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of fundamental bounds and the explicit construction of optimal protocols for channel estimation and purification are central to quantum information theory, providing concrete benchmarks for quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental limits on purifying an unknown unitary quantum channel corrupted by depolarizing noise, and analyzes the dual problem of noisy unitary conjugation.

**Main result.** The optimal query complexity for purification is found to be $\Theta(d^2 p / \epsilon)$, achieving a scaling better than naive methods, and the optimal fidelity for purification matches that of conjugation in the low-noise limit.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques including the Choi matrix formalism, semidefinite programming (SDP) to establish lower bounds, and constructing explicit $\mathrm{SU}(d)$-covariant parallel protocols to saturate these bounds.

**Model / system.** The system involves unknown $d$-dimensional unitary channels ($U \in SU(d)$) subjected to depolarizing noise ($D_p$). The goal is to design a superchannel that reconstructs $U$ from noisy measurements.

**Key observables.** Channel fidelity ($f_{	ext{Choi}}$), first-order infidelity coefficient ($C_{n,d}$), and the query complexity ($\Theta(d^2 p / \epsilon)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($p$), dimension ($d$), and number of uses/queries ($n$). The analysis focuses on the low-noise ($p 	o 0$) and large-query ($n 	o \infty$) limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assuming the use of the 'twirled protocol' for universality and utilizes complex algebraic machinery involving Schur transforms and Young's orthogonal representation for deriving bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the quantum superchannel for purification, the relationship between Young diagrams, and the Stinespring circuit diagram used in the fidelity calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem formulation, derives fundamental lower bounds using SDP (Theorem 1), constructs an explicit $\mathrm{SU}(d)$-covariant protocol to achieve an upper bound (Theorem 2), and then analyzes the dual problem of noisy unitary conjugation, showing the results coincide.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the problem of noisy unitary purification. Given access to an unknown $d$-dimensional unitary channel followed by depolarizing noise of strength $p$, we aim to construct a superchannel that universally purifies the noisy unitary back to the original unknown unitary. We optimize over arbitrary adaptive sequential strategies and analytically derive the optimal fidelity to the leading order in the noise strength and number of channel uses, while also providing a concrete $\mathrm{SU}(d)$-covariant parallel strategy that attains the optimum. Our result implies the query complexity $Θ(d^2p/ε)$ for achieving leading-order infidelity $ε$ in the low-noise regime, which scales better than the naive approach combining optimal state purification and storage-and-retrieval of quantum channels. We also consider the dual problem of noisy unitary conjugation, where the goal is to obtain the best approximation of the complex conjugate of the original unknown unitary from access to noisy queries. We show that the optimal fidelity for this task coincides with that of noisy unitary purification to the leading-order in the low-noise and large-query limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, unified projective-geometric method using grids on dual quadrics to systematically construct Kochen-Specker configurations. It shows that this single mechanism can generate numerous known examples of quantum contextuality, including those related to $F_4$ and $H_4$. The work provides deep mathematical insight into the structure of quantum contextuality.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly mathematical, the core subject—quantum contextuality—is fundamental to quantum foundations and has implications for understanding measurement limitations in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a systematic geometric framework to construct and organize Kochen-Specker (KS) configurations, which are mathematical realizations of quantum contextuality.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that several prominent contextual configurations (like those related to $F_4$ and $H_4$) arise from a single, unified projective-geometric mechanism involving grids on dual quadrics in $\mathbb{P}^3(\mathbb{R})$.

**Method.** The construction uses finite grids on pairs of smooth quadrics related by Euclidean polarity, employing a 'polar-secant principle' to generate orthogonal measurement contexts.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum contextuality, mathematically represented by sets of rays (vectors) in a Hilbert space that must satisfy specific constraints (contextuality) regardless of the measurement context chosen.

**Key observables.** Orthogonal contexts (sets of four pairwise orthogonal rays), the existence of infinite families of KS configurations, and the recovery of known exceptional root configurations ($F_4, H_4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Odd integers $p, q \geq 3$ used to define the grid parameters $Z_{p,q}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the properties of Euclidean polarity and the parity criterion for KS configurations; later sections test assumptions about complete intersections in projective space.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure involves geometric constructions on quadrics and verification of algebraic relationships.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general geometric framework (quadrics and polarity) to construct an infinite family of configurations ($Z_{p,q}$), then analyzes specific cases (Cabello, $F_4, H_4$), and finally verifies advanced structural properties using computational algebra (e.g., checking for complete intersections).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a geometric framework for constructing and organizing Kochen--Specker configurations in real four-dimensional space. The construction uses finite grids on pairs of smooth quadrics related by Euclidean polarity. Their incidence geometry directly produces orthogonal measurement contexts and parity proofs of quantum contextuality, yielding infinite families of configurations and a geometric interpretation and extension of a previously known cyclic construction. For a distinguished subfamily, the same geometry admits a canonical completion determined by secant lines. The first two instances of this completion recover the exceptional root configurations of types $F_4$ and $H_4$, while the smallest case also recovers the Cabello configuration and its embedding in the Peres configuration. Thus several prominent four-dimensional contextual configurations, previously obtained from different constructions, arise from a single projective-geometric mechanism.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework for analyzing percolation on hypergraphs where nodes have distinct functional roles (anchors vs. non-anchors). By deriving self-consistency equations, the authors map out the phase diagram governing the existence of a giant core, showing that node heterogeneity can fundamentally change the transition from gradual to catastrophic collapse.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical network theory, the study of critical transitions, phase diagrams, and structural robustness in complex systems shares mathematical parallels with phase transitions studied in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a comprehensive theoretical framework for $(k,n)$-core percolation models on hypergraphs that accounts for functional heterogeneity in node roles.

**Main result.** The study derives self-consistency equations for first- and second-neighbor pruning processes, revealing how node heterogeneity and extended interactions affect network robustness, leading to phase diagrams with continuous and discontinuous transitions.

**Method.** The authors use the cavity approach to derive self-consistency equations for the percolation process, comparing analytical predictions with numerical simulations on random hypergraphs.

**Model / system.** The model is based on hypergraphs $H = (V, E)$, where nodes can be designated as 'anchor nodes' with probability $	heta$. The core structure is defined by simultaneous constraints: a node must belong to at least $k$ active hyperedges, and a hyperedge must retain at least $n$ active nodes.

**Key observables.** The size of the giant $(k, n)$-core, and the nature of the phase transition (continuous vs. discontinuous).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Anchor probability ($	heta$), minimum node degree ($k$), minimum hyperedge size ($n$), and the connectivity range (first-neighbor vs. second-neighbor).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Nodes independently become anchor nodes with probability $	heta$. The analysis covers first-neighbor and second-neighbor pruning processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams in the $\langle q angle-\langle m angle$ plane, showing phase boundaries separating percolating/non-percolating regimes for second-neighbor pruning.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the model, derives self-consistency equations for first-neighbor and second-neighbor pruning, analyzes limiting cases ($	heta=0, 	heta=1$), and concludes by discussing the implications of functional heterogeneity on network robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hypergraphs describe higher-order interactions that involve more than a pair of nodes. A characteristic feature of hypergraphs is that their robustness can be strongly affected by the different roles of the nodes. Indeed, some nodes might be essential for a hyperedge's function, while others might not be. The loss of a single essential node completely destroys the hyperedge it belongs to, while the loss of a non-essential node has a buffering effect, inducing the hyperedge to simply reduce its size. In order to capture this phenomenology, we formulate a comprehensive theoretical framework for $(k,n)$-core percolation models on hypergraphs, where each node of a hyperedge is an anchor with probability $θ$, and a hyperedge fails if an anchor node fails. Hypergraph $(k,n)$-core percolation problems can be classified as first-neighbor and second-neighbor problems, indicating that in the pruning process the connectivity is ensured only by the state of the first neighbors or the second neighbors, respectively. We derive self-consistency equations for first-neighbor and second-neighbor (node- and hyperedge-based) pruning processes, and obtain the size of the giant $(k,n)$-core. We obtain the phase diagram, including continuous and discontinuous transitions, and confirm our theory on random hypergraphs using numerical simulations. The results show how the heterogeneity of the nodes' functional roles and the extended range of the interactions affect the robustness of higher-order networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores novel magnetic domain walls in rhombohedral graphene, which possess both spin and valley degrees of freedom. By modeling the system using Hartree-Fock theory, the authors identify two distinct wall types whose existence depends on the competition between Hund coupling and SOC. Crucially, they show that the dynamics of these textures can couple to superconducting transport, suggesting potential signatures for quantum electronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The coupling of magnetic texture dynamics to superconducting transport (generating a voltage) provides a mechanism for reading out complex quantum states using superconducting junctions, which is highly relevant for quantum device physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of qualitatively new spatial magnetic textures, specifically domain walls, in rhombohedral graphene due to the interplay of spin and valley degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The research uncovers two distinct classes of domain walls—a two-component and an intrinsically multicomponent (four-component) wall—whose existence is controlled by the competition between intervalley Hund's coupling and spin-orbit coupling.

**Method.** The study employs a microscopic approach using the self-consistent Hartree–Fock (HF) Hamiltonian, supplemented by continuum $CP^3$ theory for analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is rhombohedral multilayer graphene, analyzed in the valley-imbalanced quarter-metal phase. The model incorporates spin-valley-momentum (SWMc) band structure, intrinsic SOC, and Hund's exchange interactions.

**Key observables.** Local charge density, spin-valley order parameters ($\langle s_i \omega_j angle$), and the superconducting phase difference coupling to the texture's internal phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio between intervalley Hund's coupling ($g_{\perp}|n_e|$) and spin-orbit coupling ($\lambda_{soc}$), which dictates the transition between the two wall types.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the HF approximation and, when using continuum theory, assumes a uniform local carrier density and a rank-one projector for the order parameter.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spatial profiles of order parameters for both two-component and four-component walls, demonstrating the continuous development of transverse spin and valley coherence above a critical coupling ratio.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical problem in rhombohedral graphene, detailing the microscopic HF calculations, characterizing the two distinct domain wall regimes based on coupling strengths, and finally exploring the coupling of these textures to superconducting transport.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spatial textures of magnetic order, such as domain walls and skyrmions, are fundamental objects in magnetism. In rhombohedral multilayer graphene, magnetic order involves spin and valley degrees of freedom, opening the possibility of qualitatively new spatial textures. Here, we explore this possibility through a microscopic study of a one-dimensional domain wall in the valley-imbalanced quarter-metal phase of rhombohedral graphene. We uncover two different classes of domain walls. One resembles a conventional magnetic domain wall, locally rotating between the two bulk states, whereas the other is intrinsically multicomponent and explores states that are not occupied in either bulk domain. Which texture is realized is controlled by the competition between intervalley Hund's coupling and spin-orbit coupling, and we identify experimental signatures to distinguish them. We further show that, in a superconducting junction formed across the wall, the superconducting phase difference couples directly to the intervalley-coherent phase of the texture. Precession of this internal phase can therefore generate a voltage across the junction. Our theory shows that rhombohedral graphene indeed has magnetic textures beyond conventional magnet and that their dynamics can couple to superconducting transport.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the concept of the modular commutator, originally used to measure the chiral central charge in gapped topological phases, to gapless systems. The key finding is that at a Dirac node, the commutator yields a robust half-quantized value, which the authors interpret as the entanglement signature of the parity anomaly. This provides a novel, information-theoretic measurement tool for topological properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between entanglement measures (modular commutator) and fundamental topological invariants (parity anomaly, Chern number) provides a powerful, information-theoretic tool for characterizing topological phases, which is highly relevant to modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates what the modular commutator measures when the 2D bulk state transitions from a gapped phase to a gapless phase, generalizing its utility beyond gapped systems.

**Main result.** For a single Dirac node, the modular commutator exhibits a robust half-quantized value, which is proposed to be the entanglement manifestation of the parity anomaly. This quantization is lost in other types of gaplessness like Fermi surfaces.

**Method.** The analysis uses the modular commutator $J(A,B,C)$ formalism, comparing its behavior in gapped versus gapless regimes, and employing symmetry arguments and correlation function analysis.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the free-fermion Haldane honeycomb model, studied at its critical point hosting an isolated Dirac node. The analysis also considers systems with chiral edge modes and compares results to quadratic nodes and Fermi surfaces.

**Key observables.** Modular Commutator ($J$), Chiral Central Charge ($c^-$), Half-quantized value (1/2), Hall conductance ($\sigma_{xy}$), and Conditional Mutual Information ($	ext{CMI}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dirac velocity, Dirac cone anisotropy, system size (for finite-size corrections), and energy cutoff ($\Lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the free-fermion approximation and assumes that the half-quantization is robust against tripartition deformation for the Dirac node case.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup using the Haldane model, showing the MC converging to 0.5 at the critical point, and comparing entanglement scaling for different gapless systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from the gapped case where $J$ measures $c^-$, to the gapless Dirac node case where $J$ is half-quantized, and finally generalizes the analysis by comparing this behavior to other gapless scenarios and relating it to the parity anomaly.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The modular commutator $J(A,B,C) = i\langle[K_{AB},K_{BC}]\rangle$ extracts the chiral central charge $c_-$ from a single bulk wavefunction of a \emph{gapped} 2d state, where $3J/π=c_-$. Inspired by the recent developments in the field of gapless symmetry-protected topological phases, we ask: what does the modular commutator measure, if it is well-defined at all, when the 2d bulk becomes \textit{gapless}? Several interesting new insights can already be obtained using the simple Haldane honeycomb model. For the critical point hosting an isolated Dirac node we find that $J$ remains sharp: it converges to a \textit{half-quantized} value, with corrections that decay as a power law in the subsystem size rather than exponentially, mirroring the power-law correlations in gapless systems. We prove the half-quantization using an emergent reflection symmetry of the massless Dirac cone, and show that the half-quantized contribution comes from the other gapped cone (the massive partner of the massless one). This massive partner can be interpreted as the physical incarnation of the Pauli-Villars regulator, which is the origin of the parity-breaking level-$\frac{1}{2}$ Chern-Simons term (with half-quantized Hall conductance) and the parity anomaly. When protected chiral edge modes coexist with a bulk Dirac node we obtain $3J/π= c_-+\frac{1}{2}$. The half-quantization is also shown to be robust against tripartition deformation, tuning Dirac velocity and Dirac cone anisotropy. We further investigate other types of gaplessness---quadratic nodes (in contrast to linear Dirac) and the case with Fermi surface---and show that the robust half-quantization of $J$ is lost in such non-Dirac cases. These results generalize the modular commutator beyond gapped phases, and at the same time provide an information-theoretic measurement of the parity anomaly.

</details>

<a id="paper-2608.25870"></a>
### [Surpassing Gaussian optimality in multiparameter estimation with indefinite causal order](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25870v1)

**Authors:** Sudipta Das, Rivu Gupta, Aditi Sen De, Himadri Shekhar Dhar

**Summary.** This work investigates enhancing quantum parameter estimation in continuous-variable systems by analyzing optimal probe states for estimating displacement and squeezing. The authors demonstrate that while Gaussian probes are optimal under standard assumptions, incorporating indefinite causal order (ICO) allows for surpassing the Gaussian precision limit. This highlights ICO as a novel, independent resource for quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly tackles fundamental limits in quantum sensing, showing how non-standard resources like ICO can enhance metrological performance beyond what is achievable with simple Gaussian states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the precision of simultaneous multiparameter estimation (displacement and squeezing) in continuous-variable quantum systems beyond the standard Gaussian optimality limits.

**Main result.** Introducing indefinite causal order (ICO) in the protocol can surpass the Gaussian precision bound, and this advantage is shown to be independent of non-Gaussianity.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum metrology frameworks, calculating bounds via the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) and comparing the standard SLD-CRB against the Holevo-Cramér-Rao bound (HCRB).

**Model / system.** The system involves single-mode Gaussian probes generated by displacement and squeezing operations on the vacuum state, analyzed in the context of continuous-variable quantum systems.

**Key observables.** Estimation precision (quantified by the CRB) for displacement ($\alpha$) and squeezing ($r$) parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Probe energy ($E_P$), squeezing strength ($\zeta$), and the parameters defining the unknown transformations ($\alpha, r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares the SLD-CRB to the HCRB, and the optimal Gaussian probe state is found to be agnostic to the operation ordering.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the SLD-CRB versus encoding squeezing strength ($r$) for different probe energies, showing the optimal Gaussian bound ($C_{G,opt}$) and the displaced-squeezed bound ($C_{DS}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes Gaussian probe optimality first, followed by dedicated sections studying the introduction of ICO in probe preparation and ICO in parameter encoding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify single-mode Gaussian probes, generated by displacement and squeezing operations on the vacuum state, which are optimal for the simultaneous estimation of displacement and squeezing operations in continuous-variable quantum systems. Importantly, our results reveal that the best precision at a fixed energy is achieved not by an experimentally costly squeezing resource, but rather by redirecting some of the energy towards displacement, thus allowing for more resource-effective operations. Furthermore, introducing indefinite causal order (ICO) in either the probe preparation or parameter encoding step can surpass the Gaussian precision bound, even though the optimal Gaussian probe state is agnostic to the ordering of the operations. Specifically, we observe that odd-parity superpositions of the two definite orders can enhance precision over optimal Gaussian probes in specific parameter regimes. Further, the observed advantage cannot be attributed solely to non-Gaussianity, as quantified by the relative entropy of non-Gaussianity, highlighting ICO as an independent resource for enhancing multiparameter estimation.

</details>

**Authors:** Andreas Bluhm, Gereon Koßmann, Martin Sandfuchs, René Schwonnek, Giuseppe Viola, Ramona Wolf  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25915v1>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive mathematical toolbox for calculating key rates in one-sided device-independent Quantum Key Distribution (DIQKD). By extending the NPA hierarchy and using advanced algebraic techniques, the authors solve the key rate problem as a complex optimization over C*-algebras. The results highlight that the security performance is critically dependent on the asymmetric placement of trust between the two communicating parties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework developed—linking key rate computation to C*-algebraic optimization—provides powerful, general tools applicable to analyzing security in various quantum communication protocols beyond simple qubit systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper focuses on developing general tools and methods to calculate the achievable secret key rates for one-sided device-independent quantum key distribution (DIQKD).

**Main result.** The key rate calculation is shown to be highly dependent on which party's device is characterized, demonstrating no universal ordering for optimal trust placement.

**Method.** The authors develop extensions of the NPA hierarchy and utilize the Generalized Entropy Accumulation Theorem (GEAT) to formulate the key rate problem as a non-commutative polynomial optimization over C*-algebras.

**Model / system.** The model is the one-sided DIQKD setting, characterized by an asymmetric trust model where one party's device is fully characterized while the other is treated as a black box.

**Key observables.** Achievable secret key rate ($r_\infty$), conditional von Neumann entropy ($H(S|IQE)_ho - H(S|IB)_ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of which party (Alice or Bob) is characterized; the size of the key ($n$) for finite-size bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The public information ($I$) must not depend on the memory of the untrusted device; the analysis relies on advanced algebraic structures like C*-algebras and the NPA hierarchy extensions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates different levels of device characterization in QKD (Device-dependent, DIQKD, One-sided DIQKD). Figure 2 shows the setup for one-sided DIQKD, distinguishing between the characterized and black-boxed measurement devices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the one-sided DIQKD setting and the key rate optimization problem (using entropy bounds) to developing advanced mathematical tools. These tools involve extending the NPA hierarchy and establishing rigorous algebraic isomorphisms between the measurement constraints and tensor products of algebras.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The defining feature of one-sided device-independent quantum key distribution is its asymmetric trust model in which only one party is characterized. This scenario sets an interesting middle ground between high key rates achievable by characterizing devices and the security of full device-independence. Here, we provide new tools, methods, and benchmarks for calculating key rates in this setting. To achieve this, we develop and compare two extensions of the NPA hierarchy and derive finite-size security bounds against general attacks with arbitrary device memory. The latter is based on the Generalized Entropy Accumulation Theorem. We then investigate the performance of various protocols: the BB84 protocol both with and without losses, a qutrit mutually unbiased bases protocol, and protocols based on Bell inequalties such as CHSH and $I_{3322}$. We find that the choice of which party is characterized can strongly affects the key rate, surprisingly without a universal ordering. Our work thus offers a general toolbox for calculating key rates for one-sided device-independent QKD protocols.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25103v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work examines how well the conformal symmetry is preserved when using the derivative expansion approximation within the Functional Renormalization Group for critical systems like the 3-d Ising model. By systematically increasing the order of the approximation up to $O(\partial^4)$, the authors demonstrate that the violation of conformal constraints decreases, strongly suggesting the convergence of the approximation scheme.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on critical phenomena, the rigorous study of symmetry restoration and approximation convergence techniques (like PMS/PMC) is highly relevant to understanding effective field theories in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the convergence of the conformal Ward identity when using the derivative expansion approximation within the functional renormalization group framework.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that increasing the order of the derivative expansion systematically reduces the breaking of conformal invariance, providing evidence for the convergence of this approximation scheme.

**Method.** The study employs the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) coupled with a Derivative Expansion (DE) approximation, analyzing constraints derived from conformal Ward identities at orders up to $O(\partial^4)$.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the 3-d Ising universality class, modeled via $\phi^4$ theory near its critical point, where conformal invariance is expected.

**Key observables.** Universal critical exponents ($
u, \omega$), the degree of conformal symmetry breaking, and the function $g(ho, ar{\alpha})$ measuring constraint improvement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regulator parameter $\alpha$, the order of the derivative expansion (up to $O(\partial^4)$), and the scale-dependent cutoff $k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the DE is a systematically improvable approximation, and the analysis relies on specific regulator choices (e.g., exponential or Wetterich regulators).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the improvement of constraints ($g(ho, ar{\alpha})$) and the reduction of symmetry violations as higher orders of the derivative expansion are included, comparing results to conformal bootstrap values.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the FRG framework and the DE ansatz, then analyzing the conformal constraints at increasing orders, culminating in quantitative comparisons showing the convergence trend.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conformal invariance is expected to be an emergent property of many systems in their critical regime. However, approximation schemes generically spoil this property. This is in particular the case of the derivative expansion, a widely used approximation scheme in the framework of the functional renormalization group. In this article, we consider Ward identities associated with conformal invariance in the 3-d Ising universality class with truncations at order 4 (next-to-next-to-leading order) in the derivative expansion, with $Z_2$ invariant composite operators. Our results confirm that the regulating functions which yield a small breaking of conformal invariance also present a small sensitivity of the universal critical exponents with the choice of this regulating function. We also show that, in the vicinity of regulator-parameter values for which the conformal constraints are best satisfied, the breaking of conformal invariance reduces as the order of the derivative expansion is increased, providing a new indication of the convergence of this approximation scheme.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines defect states in 3D photonic band gap crystals, showing that defects create localized modes within the gap. The study rigorously compares finite-size effects with the infinite-crystal limit using Green's function techniques. It is significant for understanding how engineered disorder can lead to highly confined, long-lived photonic resonances.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how engineered disorder (defects) can create robust, long-lived quantum states (photonic band gap modes) whose properties are governed by localization physics, analogous to Anderson localization in electronic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence, properties, and localization of defect states within the photonic band gap of three-dimensional diamond crystals.

**Main result.** Defects introduce localized states inside the photonic band gap whose lifetimes increase exponentially with crystal size, while the theory also addresses the possibility of Anderson localization in the infinite limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical methods involving the Green's function formalism, solving eigenvalue problems for both finite and infinite crystal sizes, and employing the coupled-dipole method for lifetime calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a diamond crystal modeled with point scatterers, governed by the Maxwell-Helmholtz equation. The theory analyzes the impact of substitutional defects on the photonic band structure.

**Key observables.** Defect eigenfrequencies, state lifetimes ($\Gamma$), and the Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR) quantifying spatial localization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Crystal size (finite vs. infinite limit), frequency detuning ($\delta$), and the on-site Green's function of the ideal crystal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analyses often rely on the single-scattering approximation, and the theory compares results from the infinite-crystal limit with finite-size numerical simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the cubic unit cell structure, finite crystal sizes, the photonic band gap structure (DOS), and spatial profiles of representative localized states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the governing equations and the role of the Green's function in the ideal crystal, to analyzing the resonance conditions for defects, comparing finite-size effects via the coupled-dipole method, and finally extending the theory to multiple scattering and Anderson localization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We perform a theoretical study of defect states within the photonic band gap of three-dimensional diamond crystals composed of point scatterers and doped with substitutional defects. The defects introduce localized states inside the photonic band gap, whose existence conditions and eigenfrequencies are expressed in terms of the on-site Green's function of the ideal defect-free crystal. Off-site Green's functions are also calculated as function of distance and are shown to vanish within approximately two unit cells. Finite-size effects are analyzed by comparing the results obtained in the infinite-crystal limit with numerical simulations based on the coupled-dipole method. The latter not only reproduce the eigenfrequencies of the defect states within the band gap, but also provide their lifetimes originating from the finite crystal size. The lifetimes of the defect states increase exponentially with crystal size, becoming very long for large crystals. In addition to defect states in the three-dimensional photonic band gap, the defects also give rise to strongly detuned states outside the gap, which decouple from the spectrum of the ideal defect-free crystal.

</details>

**Authors:** Berat Yenilen, Markus Müller, Manuel Rispler

**Summary.** This paper develops fault-tolerant protocols to prepare the $|\sqrt{T}angle$ magic state using advanced QEC codes like the tetrahedral code. By incorporating this new gate primitive, the authors demonstrate a significant (20-30%) reduction in the required resources for synthesizing complex quantum circuits compared to using only the standard Clifford+T gate set.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract quantum computation, the rigorous analysis of noise coefficients and resource scaling is highly relevant to understanding the practical limitations and scaling of any physical quantum hardware platform (like trapped ions or superconducting qubits).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the efficiency of fine-grained quantum circuit synthesis by expanding the available set of fault-tolerant non-Clifford gates beyond the standard Clifford+T set.

**Main result.** Access to the $\sqrt{T}$ primitive reduces the average space-time cost for synthesizing Haar-random single-qubit unitaries by approximately 20%–30% compared to the standard Clifford+T gate set.

**Method.** The authors introduce flag fault-tolerant protocols for preparing the logical $|\sqrt{T}angle$ state on specific Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes and simulate the resulting circuits under noise.

**Model / system.** The work uses quantum computation models based on CSS stabilizer codes, specifically the 3D tetrahedral color code and its morphed variant, to implement fault-tolerant logical operations.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates (LERs), acceptance probabilities, and the space-time cost for synthesizing single-qubit unitaries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The physical error rate ($p$), the QEC code distance ($d=2t+1$), and the comparison between Clifford+T and Clifford+T + $\sqrt{T}$ gate sets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the $\sqrt{T}$ primitive can be prepared fault-tolerantly using the specified codes, and it models noise using circuit-level depolarizing channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate magic-state injection circuits for $|Tangle$ and $|\sqrt{T}angle$ states, and show representative codes like the Steane, Tetrahedral, and Morphed codes.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the protocols for preparing $|\sqrt{T}angle$ states on various codes, simulates these protocols under noise, and quantifies the resulting reduction in computational overhead compared to existing gate sets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magic-state injection is a standard route to realize universal fault-tolerant quantum computation. Whereas the set of Clifford gates in combination with the non-Clifford T gate is a widely used universal gate set, extending the available set of non-Clifford primitives can reduce compilation overhead, provided that the additional primitives can be prepared fault-tolerantly with competitive resource costs and at sufficiently low logical noise rates. In this work, we introduce flag fault-tolerant protocols for preparing logical $|\sqrt{\mathrm{T}} \rangle $ magic states on the 3D tetrahedral color code and its smaller morphed variant. Our simulations under circuit-level noise verify fault tolerance, quantify acceptance and logical error rates, and we reconstruct the effective logical channels of the corresponding circuits for gate injection via logical process tomography. We find that access to $\sqrt{\mathrm{T}}$ reduces the average space-time cost of synthesizing Haar-random single-qubit unitaries by approximately $20$-$30$% relative to the Clifford+T gate set across practically relevant approximation regimes. These results demonstrate how expanding the set of fault-tolerant non-Clifford primitives can improve computational efficiency and broaden the design space for universal quantum computation in the early fault-tolerant era.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the first demonstration of quantum-gravity-aided navigation that is entirely independent of GNSS signals. By integrating a quantum gravimeter with an INS, the system successfully corrected navigation drift over long maritime paths and performed high-resolution gravity surveys. This proves the viability of compact, autonomous quantum sensing for critical applications in denied or remote environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core technology is applied geodesy/navigation, the reliance on quantum sensing (atomic interferometry) for highly stable, drift-free measurements makes it relevant to quantum sensing applications in dynamic environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is maintaining accurate navigation and performing fine-scale surveying at sea when Global Navigation Satellite Systems (GNSS) are unavailable or unreliable, and unaided Inertial Navigation Systems (INS) suffer from drift.

**Main result.** The system successfully demonstrated bounded positioning accuracy at the nautical-mile level during GNSS-free navigation and achieved mGal-level agreement with gravity maps during surveying, resolving anomalies 50 times finer than the reference map.

**Method.** The authors employed gravity map matching, using a quantum gravimeter to constrain the drift of a navigation-grade INS by referencing local gravity measurements to a satellite-derived anomaly map.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid sensor package installed on a surface vessel, combining a quantum gravimeter (atomic sensor), a classical accelerometer, and a navigation-grade IMU. The system was tested in both gimbaled and strapdown configurations.

**Key observables.** Position error (DRMS at nautical-mile level), gravity anomaly agreement (mGal-level), repeatability/stability (sub-mGal), and long-term drift reduction factor ($\sim 70	imes$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 83 km maritime trajectory length, Sea State 4 conditions, $\sim 300$ m along-track anomaly resolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The navigation demonstration is claimed to be fully GNSS-independent, excluding GNSS from the entire measurement chain, including INS mechanization and map-matching protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the positioning error of the quantum-aided navigation against unaided INS drift over a maritime trajectory, and show gravity measurements overlaid on reference anomaly maps to highlight recovered fine-scale features.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the setup of the quantum gravimeter on a vessel, presents the methodology for both GNSS-free navigation and gravity surveying, and then presents the comparative results showing the performance gains and resolution capabilities of the quantum-aided system.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Global navigation satellite systems (GNSS) are often disrupted or unavailable at sea, and unaided inertial navigation systems (INS) drift without correction. Quantum-sensing-based gravity map matching offers a passive, infrastructure-free aid, but field demonstrations of GNSS-free quantum gravimetric navigation have not been reported. Here we perform gravity map matching and fine-resolution gravity survey with a mobile quantum gravimeter aboard a 29 m surface vessel. We hybridize an atomic sensor with a classical accelerometer for bias stabilization and independently mechanize a navigation-grade IMU, all installed in an uncontrolled cabin with no environmental stabilization or calibration. Operated in both gimbaled and strapdown configurations over identical traversals, the hybrid sensor corrected the inertial solution over an 83 km maritime trajectory by referencing locally measured gravity to a satellite-derived anomaly map. Gravity-aiding constrains INS drift and delivers bounded positioning at nautical-mile-level accuracy, with GNSS excluded throughout the measurement chain. In a separate GNSS-referenced mode, the same system surveyed coastal routes up to Sea State 4, achieving mGal-level agreement with gravimetric maps and sub-mGal repeatability and stability, with gimbaled and strapdown operation performing comparably. Resolved anomalies reach an along-track scale of ~300 m, 50X finer than the satellite map's half-power wavelength. A 56 h stationary test shows atom referencing lowers long-term drift ~70X versus the classical channel alone. These results provide the first same-instrument comparison of gimbaled and strapdown mobile quantum gravimetry and the first fully GNSS-independent gravity-map-matching navigation demonstration using a quantum gravimeter, pointing toward compact, autonomous-platform-ready quantum sensing for GNSS-denied maritime navigation and survey.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses high-pressure Raman spectroscopy on $	ext{Na}_2	ext{Co}_2	ext{TeO}_6$ to study its behavior as a candidate Kitaev Quantum Spin Liquid. By monitoring changes in vibrational modes up to 16.3 GPa, the researchers identified multiple pressure-induced phase transitions. These findings provide experimental evidence for tuning the quantum magnetic interactions in this strongly correlated material.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study uses high-pressure spectroscopy to probe the fundamental electronic and structural transitions in a material relevant to exotic quantum magnetism (Kitaev model), providing experimental data on tuning quantum ground states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates pressure-induced phase transitions in $	ext{Na}_2	ext{Co}_2	ext{TeO}_6$, a material candidate for a Kitaev Quantum Spin Liquid (KQSL) state.

**Main result.** Raman spectroscopy reveals that the material undergoes structural phase transitions at high pressures, notably marked by the emergence of a new Raman mode ($	ext{L}_1$) above 13.8 GPa.

**Method.** The research employed Raman spectroscopy performed inside a Diamond Anvil Cell (DAC) to probe the material's vibrational modes under extreme pressure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Na}_2	ext{Co}_2	ext{TeO}_6$, a quasi-2D magnet whose magnetic interactions are hypothesized to be governed by the Kitaev model due to strong spin-orbit coupling in the $	ext{Co}^{2+}$ ions.

**Key observables.** Changes in Raman mode frequencies, appearance/disappearance of modes, and changes in the polarization dependence of representative modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied pressure up to 16.3 GPa (and up to 20 GPa in related studies); the pressure evolution is analyzed in Low, Medium, and High Pressure regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that changes in Raman modes are indicative of underlying structural or electronic phase transitions, and that the Kitaev model is relevant for describing the magnetic interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic crystal structure, ambient pressure spectra, and waterfall plots showing the evolution of various Raman modes across the applied pressure range, highlighting mode changes at high pressure.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Raman spectra across different pressure regimes (LP, MP, HP), correlating observed spectroscopic changes (mode shifts, new peaks) with potential structural phase transitions in the KQSL candidate.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quasi-2D magnet Na2Co2TeO6 (NCTO) is a candidate for a Kitaev Quantum Spin Liquid (KQSL) state. Pressure-tuning in such materials is of interest as a potential method to tune the Kitaev exchange interactions, which are strongly dependent on bond geometry. Here we report a Raman spectroscopic study of NCTO inside a diamond anvil cell (DAC) with pressure applied up to 16.3 GPa. Based on the changes in the Raman modes, this pressure range is divided into three regions. The appearance and disappearance of several modes and changes in the polarization dependence of the representative modes, most prominently above 13.8 GPa, point to pressure-induced phase transitions in this material.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models CDW formation in rare-earth tritellurides using a 2D electron-phonon Hamiltonian. By analyzing the free energy and collective excitations, the authors predict a sequence of symmetry-breaking transitions, crucially demonstrating that the CDW state is highly susceptible to mirror-symmetry breaking. This provides a microscopic framework to explain complex experimental observations in these materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of symmetry breaking (mirror and glide) in a strongly correlated electronic state driven by lattice coupling provides a rich theoretical playground for understanding emergent symmetries in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the origin of surprising experimental observations in rare-earth tritellurides ($	ext{RTe}_3$), specifically focusing on evidence suggesting mirror-symmetry breaking associated with the onset of Charge-Density-Wave (CDW) order.

**Main result.** The model predicts a sequence of transitions ($T_{c1} 	o T^* 	o T_{c2}$) and shows that the CDW phase is intrinsically susceptible to mirror-symmetry breaking, which can be amplified by weak perturbations.

**Method.** The study employs mean-field theory, Ginzburg-Landau functional expansions, and real-space minimization techniques applied to a 2D electron-phonon model.

**Model / system.** The system is the quasi-2D rare-earth tritelluride ($	ext{RTe}_3$), modeled using a 2D electron-phonon Hamiltonian ($\hat{H} = \hat{H}_e + \hat{H}_{e-	ext{ph}} + \hat{H}_{	ext{ph}}$) for a single Te square-net plane.

**Key observables.** CDW order parameter, phonon spectrum (especially low-lying modes), mirror susceptibility ($\chi_M$), and the temperature evolution of symmetry breaking.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling ratios ($g_{\perp}/g_{\parallel}$), stiffness ratios ($K_2/K_1$), and temperature ($T_{c1}, T^*, T_{c2}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field theory, the adiabatic limit (neglecting quantum phonon fluctuations), and uses commensurate approximations for the ordering vector for numerical tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing CDW instability dependence on parameters, and temperature evolution plots detailing the sequence of transitions and the softening of specific phonon modes (Mode 3 and Mode 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the basic 2D electron-phonon model, analyzing the high-temperature CDW onset via mean-field theory, and then refining the analysis by examining the low-temperature sequence of symmetry-breaking transitions ($T^*, T_{c2}$) using advanced free energy minimization and mode analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Despite being arguably the simplest and best characterized quasi-2D charge-density-wave (CDW) systems, the rare-earth tritellurides ($R$Te$_3$) continue to yield surprising experimental results, including recent evidence suggestive of mirror-symmetry breaking associated with the onset of CDW order. Motivated by this, we consider a 2D electron-phonon model for a single Te square-net plane, which we analyze using mean-field theory. For an appropriate region of parameter space, we find a finite-temperature continuous transition from the normal state to a unidirectional CDW state with an ordering vector matching that observed experimentally. At lower temperatures, we find a second translation-symmetry-breaking transition, similar to what occurs in $R$Te$_3$ compounds with heavier rare-earth elements. In certain parameter regimes, we also find an intervening mirror-symmetry-breaking transition occurring between the two transitions described above. These results reveal an intrinsic susceptibility to mirror-symmetry breaking in the unidirectional CDW phase, which is relevant to understanding recent experiments on the $R$Te$_3$ compounds.

</details>

### [No Free Compression in Quantum Relaxations for Optimization](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25151v1)

**Authors:** Stuart Hadfield  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25151v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.25151_figures/2608.25151_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Three levels of compression limits. Majorana-specific. The exact result gives a Θ(n−1) universal worst-case margin despite Θ(n−1/2) typical behavior, together with a Gaussian-complete set of attainable two-point correlations (the covariance body). Designated observables. Standard QRAC information theory bounds the universal margin for fixed binary observables. Arbitrary random-access codes. Nayak’s bound converts width reduction into a copy cost for reliable recovery.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the resource cost of compressing classical information into quantum expectation values using Majorana fermions. It establishes that while the Majorana encoding offers a superior worst-case margin ($\Theta(1/n)$) compared to general bounds ($\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$), this compression shifts the computational cost into the restricted geometry of the expectation values themselves.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides quantitative limits on how much classical information can be compressed into quantum expectation values, which is crucial for designing efficient quantum algorithms and resource estimation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the resource trade-offs inherent in using quantum relaxations to compress classical decision variables for quantum optimization.

**Main result.** The exact universal worst-case margin for the quadratic-Majorana encoding is $\Theta(1/n)$, which is parametrically stronger than generic information-theoretic bounds of $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$.

**Method.** The authors use minimax optimization techniques, comparing the achievable margin against theoretical bounds derived from quantum random access codes (QRAC) and geometric constraints on the covariance body.

**Model / system.** The model focuses on encoding $m$ classical binary variables using $n$ qubits via Pauli correlation encodings, specifically analyzing the complete quadratic-Majorana family.

**Key observables.** Universal Margin ($\Delta_{	ext{Maj}}$), Covariance Body ($\Gamma$), expectation values of quadratic observables ($\langle A_{ab} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$), number of variables ($m$), and the scaling factors $\Theta(1/n)$ vs $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing the specific Majorana geometry against general information-theoretic bounds, and the results are highly dependent on the fixed family of observables considered.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares three levels of compression limits: Majorana-specific ($\Theta(n^{-1})$ worst-case margin), designated observables (QRAC bound), and arbitrary random-access codes (Nayak's bound).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem via the covariance body, deriving the exact Majorana margin using spectral methods, and then contrasting this result against general information-theoretic bounds derived from QRAC and random access coding theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qubit-efficient quantum relaxations compress classical decision variables into expectation values on substantially fewer qubits. We ask what resource tradeoffs this compression entails for quantum optimization. For the complete quadratic-Majorana encoding on $n$ qubits, pairwise correlators can represent $m=Θ(n^2)$ binary variables. We define the universal margin as the smallest correlator magnitude that can be guaranteed with prescribed signs for every target sign assignment. We show that it is exactly $Δ_{\rm Maj}(n)=\tan\!\left(\fracπ{4n}\right)=Θ(1/n)$, whereas uniformly random sign assignments retain $Θ(1/\sqrt n)$ target-specific margins. The stronger $1/n$ worst-case scaling is Majorana-specific. Moreover, arbitrary density operators and fermionic Gaussian states generate the same quadratic-Majorana covariance body, so non-Gaussian state resources cannot enlarge this two-point relaxation. Beyond Majoranas, standard quantum random access code bounds provide general information-theoretic baselines. For any fixed family of $m$ designated binary observables on $n$ qubits, the universal margin is at most $\sqrt{(2\ln2\;n/m)}$, while arbitrary random access decoding from $N$ copies with constant success probability above $1/2$ requires $nN=Ω(m)$. For a fixed Pauli correlation encoding required to work uniformly over all targets, maintaining a fixed nonzero decoded magnitude under smooth sign decoding therefore requires a rescaling parameter that grows as the available margin shrinks. Thus, while providing substantial qubit savings, compression can shift cost into restricted expectation value geometry, smaller expectation value magnitudes, or more demanding information recovery rather than eliminate it.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an Oscillator-Based Processing Unit (ONN) for speech analysis, aiming for ultra-low-power edge AI by processing raw, time-domain audio signals directly. It uses coupled ring oscillators whose synchronization behavior acts as a frequency filter bank to detect vocal formants. The approach demonstrates high classification accuracy while significantly reducing the computational overhead associated with traditional signal preprocessing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly applies concepts of coupled, nonlinear oscillators—a topic relevant to studying complex dynamics in AMO physics—to build a novel, low-power computational device, bridging nonlinear dynamics with AI.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an energy-efficient, oscillator-based processing unit (ONN) capable of directly processing time-dependent waveforms for tasks like formant recognition.

**Main result.** The proposed ONN architectures demonstrated high classification accuracy (up to 93-100%) for vowel recognition using raw or preprocessed time-domain signals.

**Method.** The core method uses coupled ring oscillators whose synchronization behavior is leveraged to act as a frequency-detecting filter bank, followed by a conventional classifier.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using coupled ring oscillators governed by ODEs, designed to operate in an analog, frequency-based manner, mimicking the function of a neural network layer.

**Key observables.** Oscillator frequency synchronization strength, classification accuracy (e.g., 93% on test set), and the detection of specific frequency components (formants).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequency range (scaled to MHz/GHz), coupling strengths, and the number of oscillator groups used for feature extraction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the energy efficiency gained by avoiding preprocessing (like spectrogram generation) outweighs the complexity of the oscillator circuit. The method relies on the separability of formant frequencies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate oscillator architecture, frequency sweeping demonstrating synchronization, and the final two-layer classification scheme mapping input waveforms to synchronized output states.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the ONN concept, details the ODE model and two proposed architectures (formant-based vs. raw waveform), presents results on vowel recognition, and discusses the energy efficiency gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Oscillatory neural networks have been successfully applied to a number of computing problems, such as associative memories and computationally hard optimization tasks. In this paper, we show how to use oscillators to process time-dependent waveforms with minimal or no preprocessing. Since preprocessing and first-layer processing are often the most power-hungry steps in neural networks, our findings may open new doors to simple and power-efficient edge-AI devices.

</details>
