**Summary.** This theoretical work predicts and classifies Quantum Spin Hall Crystals (QSHCs) in twisted bilayer $	ext{MoTe}_2$ at fractional filling. Using advanced mean-field calculations, the authors demonstrate a $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ QSHC state protected by specific symmetries. The research provides a detailed understanding of how intervalley interactions stabilize these topologically rich, charge-ordered phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of symmetry protection ($\mathbb{Z}_2$ vs $U(1)_v$) and the quantitative determination of energy splittings in topological phases are highly relevant to understanding emergent order in strongly correlated electronic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to predict and classify interaction-driven Quantum Spin Hall Crystals (QSHCs) that emerge at fractional filling due to the interplay between topology and spontaneous translation-symmetry breaking.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ QSHC state at $
u = -8/3$ in twisted bilayer $	ext{MoTe}_2$, which forms a complex ground state manifold organized by a $\mathbb{Z}_3$ index.

**Method.** The study employs Self-Consistent Hartree-Fock (SCHF) calculations performed in the crystal Brillouin zone, supplemented by topological characterization using Wilson loops and Chern numbers.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted bilayer $	ext{MoTe}_2$. The analysis uses continuum models and mean-field approximations to study the interplay between topology, interaction effects, and lattice ordering.

**Key observables.** Spin-Chern number ($C_s$), valley Chern numbers ($C_K, C_{K'}$), crystal order parameters ($\Phi_\mu(\mathbf{Q})$), and energy splitting ($\Delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fractional filling ($
u = -8/3$), twist angle ($	heta \approx 5^\circ$), and interaction strength ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the use of the Hartree-Fock mean-field theory, which is noted to be limited in capturing fully fractionalized topological orders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ QSHC structure, band structures for different phases (QSHC, AFMz, IVC), and phase diagrams showing the competition between these phases as a function of twist angle and interaction strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically predicts QSHCs, analyzes the $\mathbb{Z}_3$ organization of the ground state manifold based on relative valley alignment ($\delta$), and quantitatively determines the energetic preference between different topological sectors using first-order energy corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We predict and classify interaction-driven quantum spin Hall crystals (QSHCs), a class of states emerging at fractional filling through an interplay of topology and spontaneous translation-symmetry breaking. QSHCs form nearly degenerate manifolds whose members can realize distinct topological phases protected by time-reversal or valley $U(1)_v$ symmetry, with time-reversal acting nontrivially within the manifold. As a representative of this broad class of states we provide evidence for 9-fold quasi-degenerate $\sqrt{3}\times\sqrt{3}$ charge ordered QSHCs at $ν= -8/3$ of twisted bilayer MoTe$_2$ near a $5^\circ$ twist. Here a $\mathbb{Z}_3$ index organizes states related by lattice translation into three time-reversal invariant states with nontrivial $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topology and three time-reversal related doublets whose individual members spontaneously break time-reversal and carry a $U(1)_v$ protected spin-Chern number. Finally, we determine the conditions that favor QSHCs over closely competing intervalley-coherent crystals.

</details>

**Summary.** This work simulates the behavior of topologically-protected valley Hall modes in patterned bilayer graphene superlattices. Using tight-binding methods, the authors show that impurities can facilitate resonant tunneling between islands, while the topological protection remains robust against certain device imperfections. This research advances the understanding of engineered topological quantum transport in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study directly models topological edge states and their interaction with disorder (impurities), which is highly relevant for understanding robust quantum transport in mesoscopic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the scattering properties of topologically-protected valley Hall modes in engineered bilayer graphene superlattices.

**Main result.** Resonant tunneling via impurities enhances island overlap, and the topological protection remains robust even with misalignment of gate patterns.

**Method.** The study employs tight-binding simulations and analyzes the scattering matrix (S-matrix) to probe quantum transport phenomena.

**Model / system.** The system is Bernal bilayer graphene (BLG) patterned into superlattices using gate-defined islands, creating a sign-alternating gap structure that hosts valley Hall modes.

**Key observables.** Scattering probability, resonant tunneling enhancement, and the persistence of topological protection.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interlayer bias ($U_0$), impurity potential strength, and the separation distance ($L$) between superlattice islands.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the ability to engineer a device with an alternating gap structure and treats the system using a spinless, nearest-neighbor tight-binding Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device setup, the VHM profile near domain walls, and energy sweeps showing oscillatory tunneling effects dependent on island separation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using a tight-binding Hamiltonian, then analyzes the existence of topologically-protected valley Hall modes, and finally investigates how impurity scattering and island geometry affect these modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the present paper we perform a tight-binding simulation of gate-defined islands in Bernal bilayer graphene (BLG). The inversion of the gap sign on the boundaries of the islands creates topologically-protected valley Hall modes. We focus on the specific questions of whether the valley Hall modes around such islands could serve as a host for quantum walks or simulate weakly coupled systems, and how their tunneling is affected by in-gap impurities. In addition, we discuss the effect of misalignment of top and bottom gate patterns on the tunneling properties between islands. Our main results show that resonant tunneling via an impurity enhances overlap between superlattice islands, while misalignment does not break topological protection over a wide parameter regime. In addition, we study the two-island geometry and show that it is possible to leverage suppressed scattering to place islands more densely on a single sample.

</details>

**Authors:** Pavel Sekatski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25010v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

They differ only in which classical variables are exchanged between the encoder, the latent instrument and the decoder. **Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous, operational framework to define the 'effective coherent quantum dimension' of noisy quantum operations. It introduces and distinguishes three related concepts—$d$-compressibility, $d$-simulability, and $d$-embeddability—which quantify how much quantum information can be processed despite noise. The work culminates in calculating the precise noise threshold required for a universal quantum processor to maintain a specific level of coherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical tools for quantifying the operational limitations of noisy quantum hardware, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the effective coherent quantum dimension of programmable quantum operations when noise prevents coherence across the full nominal Hilbert space.

**Main result.** It establishes a strict hierarchy among three notions—$d$-embeddability $\implies d$-simulability $\implies d$-compressibility—and derives the white-noise threshold for $d$-embeddability of noisy unitary channels.

**Method.** The authors introduce an operational, basis-independent framework using decompositions involving encoders, latent instruments, and decoders to formalize the dimension bottleneck.

**Model / system.** The model involves programmable quantum devices acting on a Hilbert space of dimension $n$, characterized by sets of quantum instruments $\{I_{a|x}\}$ mapping between systems of dimensions $n$ and $m$.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the three notions of dimension bottleneck ($d$-compressibility, $d$-simulability, $d$-embeddability) and the white-noise threshold $f_2(d, n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the nominal dimension $n$, the bottleneck dimension $d$, and the noise level (related to the white-noise threshold).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption of a 'dimensional bottleneck' and, in later parts, the use of the Haar measure for random unitaries to prove optimality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural decompositions corresponding to $d$-compressibility, $d$-simulability, and $d$-embeddability for both single channels and sets of instruments.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the operational framework and defines the three notions, establishing the hierarchy. It then analyzes special cases (measurements, states) and progresses to derive the optimal decomposition strategy and calculate the exact white-noise threshold for noisy unitary channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable quantum devices nominally act on a Hilbert space whose dimension grows exponentially with the number of constituents, but the presence of noise makes it unlikely that they remain coherent across all of this immense Hilbert space. Then, what is the effective coherent quantum dimension that should be associated with such imperfect devices? To answer this question we here introduce an operational basis-independent framework which imposes a dimension bottleneck on the programmable transformations. Concretely we ask how strongly the quantum information they process can be compressed. Formalizing this idea we identify three inequivalent notions, termed $d$-compressibility, $d$-simulability and $d$-embeddability, which differ in the causal structure used to impose the bottleneck and form a strict hierarchy. The framework unifies several existing notions: joint measurability and simulability of quantum measurements, and the absolute dimensionality of state ensembles, are recovered as special cases. We illustrate the hierarchy with noisy qubit measurements in complementary bases, and we determine the white-noise thresholds at which the set of all noisy unitary channels in dimension $n$, a noisy universal quantum processor, becomes $d$-compressible, $d$-simulable and $d$-embeddable. The thresholds confirm the expectation -- maintaining coherence across the full Hilbert space becomes increasingly demanding as the nominal dimension $n$ increases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel geometric framework to determine the robustness of magic for quantum states encoded by Codeword-Stabilized codes. By developing a CWS-adapted magic-witness, the authors reduce a complex quantum optimization problem to a tractable classical affine-incidence problem. This yields precise, computable lower bounds on quantum resource robustness based purely on the geometry of the underlying classical word set.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful geometric tool (affine geometry) to analyze quantum resource bounds (magic robustness) in QEC codes, connecting abstract quantum optimization to concrete classical combinatorial problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a framework to compute lower bounds on the robustness of magic ($R(ho)$) for quantum states arising from Codeword-Stabilized (CWS) codes.

**Main result.** The quantum optimization problem is exactly reduced to a classical affine-incidence problem over the CWS word set, providing a fixed-parameter algorithm for calculating the stabilizer threshold.

**Method.** The authors use a CWS-adapted magic-witness framework to isolate codeword coherence, converting the quantum optimization into a geometric problem involving affine intersections of the classical word set.

**Model / system.** The model involves CWS codes, which provide a unified graph-state description for both stabilizer and non-additive quantum error-correcting codes. The states analyzed are coherent superpositions of individual word states.

**Key observables.** Robustness of magic ($R(ho)$), the stabilizer threshold ($eta_{	ext{coh}}$), and the affine-intersection profile $M_r(C)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the word set ($K$), the number of qubits ($n$), and the structure of the classical word set $C \subseteq \mathbb{F}_2^n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The certificate relies on calibrated Pauli measurements and is a magic witness, not a device-independent Bell inequality. The analysis is most meaningful when the target state is the coherent CWS superposition.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lower-bound principle using CHSH/W-type scans, comparing exact RoM versus the calculated lower bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the coherence witness, deriving the general bound, specializing to the equal-weight case where phase optimization collapses, and finally establishing the exact reduction to the classical affine-incidence problem.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Codeword-stabilized quantum codes give a unified graph-state description of stabilizer and nonadditive quantum error-correcting codes. Although each individual CWS word state is stabilizer, coherent superpositions of different word states can be nonstabilizer. We develop a CWS-adapted magic-witness framework that isolates this codeword coherence and converts it into certified lower bounds on robustness of magic.   The main result is an exact reduction of the stabilizer threshold of a natural CWS coherence witness to a finite-geometric problem over the classical CWS word set. For general weighted superpositions, the threshold is computed by enumerating affine intersections and affine-quadratic phases. For equal-weight superpositions, the phase optimization collapses, and the threshold is determined entirely by how many CWS words can lie in affine flats of each dimension. Thus a quantum optimization over stabilizer states becomes a classical affine-incidence problem.   This reduction yields a fixed-parameter algorithm, an analytic lower bound for an infinite union-stabilizer family, and exact rational certificates for several standard nonadditive CWS examples. The framework also clarifies why exact enumeration fails for large structured families and identifies the remaining task as an affine-intersection problem. The result provides a geometric mechanism by which nonlinear CWS word sets generate certifiable magic.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the difficult problem of decoding quantum LDPC codes by reformulating it as estimating partition functions within a Markov Random Field model. The authors introduce novel, certified decoders that surpass existing heuristic methods by providing mathematical guarantees of optimality for most decisions. This advances the practical feasibility of fault-tolerant quantum computation by improving error correction decoding.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework for handling error degeneracy in quantum codes, especially the development of certified decoding methods, provides tools applicable to understanding the limits of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is decoding quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (qLDPC) codes, which is significantly harder than classical decoding because errors are equivalent up to stabilizers, requiring the comparison of entire error class probabilities (partition functions).

**Main result.** The authors developed a certified sampling decoder that matches or exceeds existing decoders while providing formal certificates of optimality for the majority of syndrome decisions.

**Method.** The problem is reframed as probabilistic inference in an undirected graphical model (Markov Random Field) over the code's check variables, allowing for the estimation of class partition functions using techniques like Annealed Importance Sampling (AIS) and Bethe free energy approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum error correction using qLDPC codes, which are treated as an MRF. The framework generalizes concepts from the surface code to arbitrary CSS codes and spacetime decoding.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates, certified fractions of correct decisions, and the comparison of decoding performance against heuristic decoders like BP+OSD.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code structure (e.g., [[72, 12, 6]]), noise models (depolarizing, circuit-level), and the computational cost (e.g., millisecond decoding time).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the problem can be mapped to an MRF partition function estimation, and the Bethe decoder's bias cancels out when comparing different error classes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the difficulty of degenerate quantum decoding, proposes the MRF framework for partition function estimation, details two main decoders (certified sampling and region-based), and validates them across various codes and noise models, concluding with hardware implementation results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes reduce the qubit overhead of fault-tolerant quantum computation by an order of magnitude, but their decoding is harder than its classical counterpart: because many physical errors are equivalent up to stabilizers, the degenerate maximum-likelihood (ML) decoder must compare the probabilities of entire equivalence classes of errors, that is, partition functions, rather than single errors. The workhorse decoder BP+OSD sidesteps degeneracy heuristically and offers no guarantees. We treat degenerate decoding as probabilistic inference in an undirected graphical model: the probability of each logical class is the partition function of an unconstrained, strictly positive Markov random field over the code's check variables, a construction that generalizes the random-bond Ising mapping of the surface code to arbitrary CSS codes and to spacetime decoding with measurement errors and circuit-level noise. On this model we build two decoders. The first estimates all class partition functions by annealed importance sampling with common random numbers and attaches to every decision a certificate of optimality: a paired bootstrap test, or, composed with constant-factor estimators such as WISH, an exact optimality proof. The second is region-based: the Bethe free energy, whose bias cancels between classes, reproduces exact ML decoding on every tested surface-code instance at millisecond cost, and enlarging the regions to elimination clusters makes exact degenerate ML decoding of the [[72,12,6]] bivariate bicycle code feasible. Across surface codes and the bivariate bicycle codes [[72,12,6]] and [[144,12,12]], under code-capacity, phenomenological, and circuit-level noise, the sampling decoder matches or exceeds BP+OSD while certifying the bulk of its decisions, and the certificate flags exactly the syndromes on which any fast decoder should be distrusted.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a generalized, efficient quantum circuit framework for simulating discrete-time quantum walks on arbitrary Cayley graphs. By systematically decomposing the complex shift operator using a multi-stage factorization technique, the authors achieve substantial reductions in required CNOT gates compared to standard methods. This provides a crucial, hardware-conscious tool for realizing quantum walk simulations on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the resource estimation for implementing quantum walks, which are fundamental models for quantum search and simulation, making it highly relevant for quantum algorithm development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing a generalized and efficient quantum circuit framework for implementing discrete-time quantum walks (DTQWs) on Cayley graphs of arbitrary dimension.

**Main result.** The proposed multi-stage decomposition of the shift operator significantly reduces the upper-bound CNOT cost compared to naive implementations, providing a scalable pathway for near-term quantum devices.

**Method.** The authors build upon the Boundary QFT scheme by introducing a systematic, hierarchical multi-stage decomposition of the shift operator, which factorizes the QFT-diagonalized operator into structured block components.

**Model / system.** The model is the Discrete-Time Quantum Walk (DTQW) evolving on a Cayley graph $\Gamma(G, S)$. The evolution is governed by $U = S(C \otimes I_p)$, where $S$ is the shift operator, which is the primary computational bottleneck.

**Key observables.** Upper-bound CNOT cost, gate complexity, and the reduction factor compared to naive implementations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of the generating set ($k$) is identified as the dominant resource parameter, with efficiency gains shown for $k \le 64$ (inverse-closed) and $k \le 16$ (non-inverse-closed).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the linear CNOT scaling established by Rosa et al. and assumes the efficiency gain is largely insensitive to the system size $N$.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate examples of different Cayley graphs and provide a graphical representation of the DTQW setup, including specific optimized quantum circuits for 2D torus graphs.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the DTQW model and the role of the shift operator. It then details the systematic multi-stage decomposition, generalizing the Boundary QFT scheme. The core of the work involves applying this decomposition to specific graph examples (1D and 2D torus graphs) and performing gate complexity analysis to demonstrate the efficiency gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a generalized and efficient quantum circuit framework for implementing discrete-time quantum walks (DTQWs) on Cayley graphs of arbitrary dimension. Building on the Boundary QFT scheme of Razzoli et al., we introduce a systematic multi-stage decomposition of the shift operator for 1D Cayley graphs across three classes of generating sets: inverse-closed without involutions, inverse-closed with an involution, and non-inverse-closed. The decomposition hierarchically factorizes the QFT-diagonalized shift operator into structured block components, progressively reducing the control degree of the required rotation gates and replacing high-degree multi-qubit controlled operations with collections of lower-degree equivalents. We extend this construction to $d$-dimensional torus graphs and provide explicit circuit implementations for an 8-Cayley graph and a $\mathbb{Z}_{16} \times \mathbb{Z}_8$ torus graph as concrete illustrations. Gate complexity analysis using the linear CNOT scaling of Rosa et al. demonstrates that the decomposed implementation achieves a substantial reduction in upper-bound CNOT cost relative to the naive implementation within the regime $k \leq 64$ for inverse-closed graphs and $k \leq 16$ for non-inverse-closed graphs, where $k$ denotes the degree of the generating set. Benchmarking further reveals that this efficiency gain is largely insensitive to the system size $N$, identifying $k$ as the dominant resource parameter for the shift operator. These results provide a scalable and hardware-conscious pathway toward practical DTQW implementations on near-term quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work proposes using tensile-strained SiGe/Ge heterostructures to create light-hole (LH) spin qubits, overcoming limitations found in conventional heavy-hole (HH) qubits. By engineering the lattice structure, the authors achieve a significantly more isotropic spin response and a substantial increase in the qubit's quality factor. The model also demonstrates the feasibility of electrically switching between HH and LH ground states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed theoretical modeling of spin-orbit coupling and g-tensor renormalization in semiconductor heterostructures is highly relevant for designing solid-state qubits, providing concrete material parameters for quantum device engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for advanced hole-based spin qubits with improved coherence and reduced spin anisotropy compared to established heavy-hole (HH) platforms.

**Main result.** The proposed tensile-strained Light-Hole (LH) heterostructure lattice-matched to Ge successfully stabilizes an LH ground state exhibiting large spin-orbit coupling and an order-of-magnitude improvement in the quality factor.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical calculations involving the numerical diagonalization of a 6-band k.p Hamiltonian and projecting the resulting Hamiltonian to analyze spin properties.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a quantum well/barrier structure made of tensile-strained SiGe/Ge, designed to confine holes in a light-hole (LH) ground state. The model uses a k.p Hamiltonian incorporating linear and cubic spin-orbit coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Spin-orbit coupling parameters ($\alpha, eta_2, eta_3$), g-tensor components ($g^*_{\parallel}, g^*_{\perp}$), dipole moment, and Quality factor ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Well thickness ($L_z$), Ge concentration ($x_w$), and applied electric field ($F_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system remains structurally stable against strain relaxation, and calculations are performed by projecting the full Hamiltonian onto the LH ground state doublet.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy gap closure between HH and LH states, the dependence of SOC parameters and effective mass on composition/thickness, and the tunability of the g-tensor components with applied electric fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the proposed LH platform against traditional HH platforms, deriving the effective Hamiltonian, calculating key spin parameters (SOC, g-tensor), and demonstrating the advantages in coherence and tunability, culminating in the proposal of a switching bilayer structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strained germanium ($\varepsilon$-Ge) quantum wells on metamorphic SiGe buffers have enabled advanced hole-based spin qubit devices. Alternatively, unstrained Ge with lattice-matched strained silicon-germanium ($\varepsilon$-SiGe) barriers eliminates the need for metamorphic buffers altogether. The ground state character of both these platforms is predominantly heavy-hole (HH) with a largely anisotropic spin response. We propose and study an alternative heterostructure, lattice-matched to Ge, in which both the SiGe quantum well and barriers are tensile strained, with their composition contrast providing the band offset for confinement and the tensile strain stabilizing a light-hole (LH) ground state. We show large spin-orbit coupling (SOC), both linear and cubic, along with a significantly more isotropic spin response compared to strained HH qubits. We also study the decoherence properties of the proposed device, showing an appreciable gain in the quality factor compared to their HH counterparts. Finally, we propose a bilayer heterostructure that allows for electrical switching between HH and LH ground state character.

</details>

**Summary.** This research determines the antiferromagnetic ground state of the triclinic double perovskite Ca2CuWO6 using neutron diffraction. By comparing it to the tetragonal analogue, Sr2CuWO6, the authors show that the structural symmetry lowering selects a single, common magnetic ordering vector. This highlights how subtle symmetry changes can dictate the precise magnetic ground state in strongly correlated oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed case study of how crystal symmetry breaking (tetragonal to triclinic) can select a specific ground state among several theoretically possible magnetic configurations, which is a key concept in understanding magnetic phase transitions in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the antiferromagnetic ground state of the triclinic double perovskite Ca2CuWO6 and analyze how symmetry breaking affects its magnetic structure compared to the tetragonal analogue, Sr2CuWO6.

**Main result.** The magnetic ground state is collinear with equal-magnitude, antiparallel moments on the two Cu sites, and the symmetry breaking from tetragonal to triclinic structure selects a single magnetic propagation vector domain.

**Method.** The study combines neutron powder diffraction measurements to determine the magnetic structure with mean-field theoretical calculations using a supercell approach to model magnetic interactions.

**Model / system.** The system involves double perovskites (A2BB'O6) containing Jahn-Teller active Cu2+ ions, leading to quasi-two-dimensional magnetic networks mediated by W-site interactions. The analysis uses a supercell construction to compare the magnetic motifs of the triclinic and tetragonal phases.

**Key observables.** Magnetic Bragg reflections indexed by k = (1/2, 1/2, 0); ordered moment of 0.66(3) μB per Cu at 1.5 K; the selected magnetic propagation vector k-domain.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transition temperatures (T_N ~ 32 K for Ca2CuWO6); exchange interaction strengths (J values); the difference in mean-field eigenvalues (Delta k).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic interactions are dominated by W-mediated, second-neighbor superexchange pathways, and the comparison is made by mapping both structures onto a common crystallographic supercell.

**Figures summary.** Figures show NPD data confirming magnetic reflections at 1.5 K, and theoretical plots compare the lowest energy mean-field eigenvalue along the Brillouin zone path for both compounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the magnetic ordering in Ca2CuWO6 via NPD, determining the collinear structure and moment size. It then employs a supercell model and mean-field theory to compare this result with Sr2CuWO6, showing that symmetry breaking selects a specific, common magnetic motif.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the antiferromagnetic ground state of the triclinic double perovskite Ca$_2$CuWO$_6$solved by neutron powder diffraction, and analyze it in direct comparison with tetragonal Sr$_2$CuWO$_6$. Below $T_\mathrm{N} \simeq 32$ K, the magnetic Bragg reflections of Ca$_2$CuWO$_6$ are indexed by the commensurate propagation vector $\mathbf{k}=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2},0)$ in its native $P\bar{1}$ cell. Rietveld refinement yields a collinear structure with equal-magnitude, antiparallel moments on the crystallographically inequivalent Cu1 and Cu2 sites and an ordered moment of $0.66(3)~μ_{\mathrm B}$ per Cu at 1.5 K. A direct comparison with Sr$_2$CuWO$_6$ is obscured by different crystallographic settings and orientations of the cooperative Jahn-Teller elongation axes. Hence, we introduce a common crystallographic supercell through which effects of symmetry lowering from tetragonal to triclinic in the double perovskite are explored: Apparently different magnetic propagation vectors map onto the same supercell wave vector, revealing a common magnetic structure stabilized by tungsten-mediated, second-neighbor interactions. Mean field calculations further show that tetragonal symmetry preserves the degeneracy of four magnetic $\mathbf{k}$-domains in Sr$_2$CuWO$_6$, whereas the triclinic splitting of symmetry-related exchange pathways in Ca$_2$CuWO$_6$, most strongly within the Cu2 network, selects a single $\mathbf{k}$-domain. These results establish the magnetic ground state of Ca$_2$CuWO$_6$ and show how symmetry breaking selects a given ordered state without changing the underlying magnetic motif of the Sr analogue.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes quantum localization in 1D systems with long-range hopping and disorder. Using advanced perturbation theory and SUSY techniques, the authors find that the ground state exhibits a unique 'semi-localized' state for certain hopping decay rates. This is quantified by a specific set of fractal dimensions, extending the understanding of wave function statistics in disordered quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'semi-localization' and the derivation of multifractal spectra provide a detailed theoretical understanding of how disorder affects quantum wavefunctions beyond simple localization/delocalization transitions, which is relevant for understanding transport in disordered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the localization properties of a quantum particle in a 1D system subjected to long-range hopping and diagonal disorder, focusing on the ground state in momentum space.

**Main result.** For specific hopping decay rates ($1 < a < 3/2$), the ground state exhibits 'semi-localization' rather than conventional localization, characterized by a specific set of fractal dimensions $D_q$.

**Method.** The analysis employs weak-disorder perturbation theory in the momentum basis, supplemented by techniques utilizing supersymmetric (SUSY) structure decomposition and asymptotic analysis of wavefunction moments.

**Model / system.** The model is a 1D quantum system described by a Hamiltonian with long-range hopping amplitudes $t(r) \propto r^{-a}$ and on-site disorder $\epsilon_i$. The study focuses on the ground state properties.

**Key observables.** Generalized fractal dimensions ($D_q$), normalized momentum-space intensity ($ho_K$), and the zero-momentum mode weight ($ho_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hopping decay exponent $a$, which dictates the behavior; the disorder strength $W$; and the hybridization parameter $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on weak-disorder perturbation theory (up to second order in $W$) and asymptotic limits for large system size $L$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Exact Diagonalization (ED) results with Perturbation Theory (PT) predictions for the energy spectrum, moment ratios, and the fractal dimensions $D_q$ across different disorder strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the Hamiltonian and the physical problem, applying SUSY and perturbation theory to derive expressions for wavefunction moments, and finally analyzing the asymptotic behavior of these moments to determine the fractal dimensions $D_q$ in different regimes of the parameter $a$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the localization of a quantum particle in a one-dimensional disordered system with long-range hopping amplitudes $t(r)\propto r^{-a}$. In contrast to the standard one-dimensional Anderson model ($a\to\infty$), in which all states are localized and the localization length is minimal at the band edge, the long-range model with $1<a<3/2$ exhibits a disorder-driven transition at the band edge, while high-energy states remain localized at arbitrary disorder strength. We investigate this transition for the ground state in momentum space. In the weak-disorder regime, we derive perturbative expressions for the characteristic functions and moments of the momentum-space wave function, as well as for its fractal dimensions. Our results demonstrate that the ground state exhibits $\it semilocalization$ rather than conventional localization, thereby extending the class of models displaying the unusual $\it semifractality$ of wave functions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the fundamental cryptographic question of whether quantum indistinguishability obfuscation (qIO) can be secured using standard, falsifiable computational assumptions. By proving an impossibility result under specific reduction constraints, the authors show that qIO security for QMA cannot be based on these assumptions. This establishes a significant theoretical barrier for constructing such primitives.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the concepts of quantum obfuscation and complexity separation are foundational to understanding the limits of quantum cryptography, which underpins many advanced quantum information protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to separate the security of Quantum Indistinguishability Obfuscation (qIO) and Witness Encryption (WE) for QMA from standard, falsifiable cryptographic assumptions.

**Main result.** It proves that the security of WE for QMA cannot be based on any falsifiable cryptographic assumption under a restricted class of quantum black-box reductions, thereby establishing a structural barrier for qIO security.

**Method.** The proof utilizes a restricted class of quantum black-box reductions and relies on the existence of a QMA–QCIP[2] gap problem as a necessary assumption.

**Model / system.** The work operates within the framework of quantum computation, focusing on quantum circuits, the complexity class QMA, and cryptographic primitives like qIO and WE.

**Key observables.** The advantage of the qIO-adversary, which measures the success probability of breaking the obfuscation scheme.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The security parameter $n$, and the assumption of a QMA–QCIP[2] gap problem.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is restricted to WE with classical ciphertexts and relies on the existence of the QMA–QCIP[2] gap problem; it does not cover all possible reductions or quantum state outputs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the formal definitions for null-qIO and WE for QMA, then constructs the restricted null-qIO reduction, culminating in a theorem that proves the separation between the security of qIO and falsifiable assumptions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum indistinguishability obfuscation (qIO) aims to make a quantum circuit unintelligible while preserving its functionality. It serves as a foundational primitive for advanced applications, such as witness encryption (WE) for QMA, non-interactive zero-knowledge arguments for QMA, and attribute-based encryption for BQP. Despite its importance, constructing qIO from standard assumptions remains a major open problem.   In this work, we prove that the security of WE for QMA cannot be based on any falsifiable cryptographic assumption via a restricted class of quantum black-box reductions. Because qIO for null quantum circuits implies WE for QMA, this also separates null-qIO from falsifiable assumptions. Since almost all standard cryptographic assumptions are falsifiable, our result presents a barrier to basing qIO on standard cryptographic assumptions.   The reductions we rule out are restricted: the reduction must query the adversary classically, non-adaptively, at the same security parameter, and only on honestly generated ciphertexts. Moreover, our impossibility applies only to WE with classical ciphertexts, and therefore does not rule out qIO with obfuscators whose output is a quantum state. Ruling out more general reductions, as well as more general forms of WE and qIO, remains open.   Our impossibility relies on the existence of a QMA-QCIP[2] gap problem, an average-case assumption postulating a QMA language that cannot be verified with two messages of classical communication.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the superadditivity of classical communication over quantum channels by replacing continuous random unitaries with discrete random permutations. This transformation converts the problem into a combinatorial one, allowing for asymptotic derandomization. While the theory provides powerful structural tools, the required system sizes for explicit counterexamples remain computationally infeasible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into fundamental capacity theorems in quantum information theory, showing how continuous random matrix theory results can be translated into discrete combinatorial statements, which is a major theoretical advance.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the structural explanation for the superadditivity of classical communication over quantum channels, which was first established using concentration of measure for Haar random unitaries.

**Main result.** The authors show that the continuous problem over unitaries can be replaced by a discrete combinatorial problem over random permutations, leading to asymptotic derandomization of the nonadditivity proof.

**Method.** The approach involves replacing Haar randomness with random permutations, utilizing theorems like Bordenave and Collins, and employing asymptotic constructions derived from strong convergence in free probability theory.

**Model / system.** The study concerns finite-dimensional quantum channels $\Phi: D(\mathcal{H}_A) 	o D(\mathcal{H}_B)$, analyzed using the random-subspace model. The core comparison is between the minimum output von Neumann entropy of the tensor product channel versus the sum of individual minimum entropies.

**Key observables.** Minimum output von Neumann entropy ($H_{\min}$), Holevo information ($\chi$), and the comparison $H_{\min}(\Phi_1 \otimes \Phi_2)$ vs $H_{\min}(\Phi_1) + H_{\min}(\Phi_2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required system size $N$ for a finite counterexample is astronomically large ($N \le 5.422 	imes 10^{116216}$), indicating the difficulty of practical construction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the limiting geometry responsible for nonadditivity is preserved when moving from continuous Haar randomness to discrete random permutations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the connection between Haar randomness and random permutations, then providing asymptotic constructions for derandomization, and finally presenting explicit, albeit numerically enormous, finite-dimensional counterexamples to superadditivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Since Hastings' proof of superadditivity of classical communication over quantum channels, considerable effort has been devoted to finding a structural explanation of this phenomenon that was originally established by concentration of measure for Haar random unitaries.   The main observation of this work is that Haar randomness can be replaced by random permutations without changing the limiting geometry responsible for nonadditivity. This replacement turns a continuous problem over unitary matrices into a discrete combinatorial problem over zero--one permutation matrices, and thereby opens a path toward derandomization.   The theorem of Bordenave and Collins shows that random permutations have the required limiting behavior and the algorithm of O'Donnell and Wu then provides a deterministic asymptotic construction, running in polynomial time in the size when the channel parameters and accuracy are fixed. Thus the random construction can be derandomized in an asymptotic algorithmic sense, although finding a simple closed-form or practically computable counterexample remains open.   Finally, a quantitative random permutation estimate by Chen, Garza-Vargas, Tropp and van Handel gives a fully numerical estimate: there exists a tuple of 57,836,025 permutations acting on a set of size \[ N \le 5.422\times 10^{116216}\] such that the associated finite dimensional channel exhibits nonadditivity. This enormous value remains an obstacle to a practical construction.

</details>

**Authors:** Zoltán György, Dmitry Miserev, Jelena Klinovaja, Daniel Loss  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25914v1>

The red circles show first-order charge noise sweet spots. The confinement frequen- cies are ℏωx = 2 meV, ℏωy = 2.6 meV, and the barrier width is d = 3 nm (see Fig. 1).</sub>

At the g-factor peak, the qubit has a fully hybridized HH-LH state, with 50% HH fraction. The confinement frequencies are ℏωx = 2 meV and ℏωy = 2.6 meV, the barrier width is d = 3 nm. (b) 2DHG spectrum at the gate voltage corresponding to the g-factor peak, Vg = 602.7 mV. The blue curves are heavy- hole subbands, and the orange curves are light-hole subbands. There is also substantial overlap between these wavefunctions in the thin Ge well.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel switchable heavy-hole/light-hole spin qubit architecture in a Ge heterostructure. By shuttling the hole state between HH and LH regimes, the device combines the robustness of HH qubits with the strong spin-orbit coupling of LH qubits. Theoretical calculations predict high coherence times ($100 \mu$s) and fast gate operations, advancing the feasibility of solid-state quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the hardware realization of solid-state qubits, providing theoretical predictions for optimizing coherence times and control mechanisms in semiconductor quantum dots, which is central to quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a highly controllable and switchable spin qubit by combining the best features of heavy-hole (HH) and light-hole (LH) spin qubits.

**Main result.** The authors propose a switchable HH-LH qubit in a bilayer Ge heterostructure that achieves a coherence time significantly longer than the LH regime while maintaining fast gate operation.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling based on the 6-band Luttinger-Kohn-Bir-Pikus Hamiltonian, incorporating quantum well and in-plane confinement potentials.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a bilayer Ge heterostructure with SiGeSn barriers, allowing the hole qubit character to be tuned between HH and LH states via gate voltage shuttling.

**Key observables.** Effective qubit g-factors ($g_x, g_y, g_z$), coherence time ($T_2$), and Rabi frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltages ($V_g$), in-plane confinement strengths ($\omega_x, \omega_y$), and the material composition of the barriers (SiGeSn).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model uses an anisotropic harmonic potential for in-plane confinement and approximates the plunger gate potential; linear interpolation for Luttinger parameters is noted as potentially inaccurate.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the heterostructure geometry, quantum well confinement profiles for HH and LH states as a function of gate voltage, and plots of effective qubit g-factors versus gate voltage, highlighting the HH-LH resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of combining HH and LH advantages, models the system using the LKBP Hamiltonian, details the gate-tunable switching mechanism, and presents calculations showing enhanced coherence and resonance features.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Compressively strained Ge quantum wells in planar SiGe/Ge heterostructures are the state-of-the-art platform for hole spin qubits. While they exhibit robust coherence times, they possess weak intrinsic spin-orbit interaction (SOI) due to the heavy-hole (HH) character of the wavefunction. Recently, light-hole (LH) qubits were proposed in GeSn/Ge heterostructures, offering strong, intrinsic, linear-in-momentum SOI. In this work, we propose a switchable HH-LH spin qubit in a bilayer Ge heterostructure with SiGeSn barriers, combining the advantages of HH and LH devices. The character of the qubit can be changed by shuttling from an LH well to an HH well, which also enables fast, hopping-based single-qubit rotations. Additionally, we observe an HH-LH resonance introduced by the in-plane confinement, resulting in $g$-factor peaks and first-order charge noise sweet spots. Our calculations reveal a sweet spot with Rabi frequencies on the order of 100 MHz, comparable to the LH regime, but with a more than tenfold increase in coherence time, on the order of 100 $μ$s.

</details>

**Authors:** Xin-Jie Liu, Shengbiao Sun, Ke-Fan Song, Jia-Peng Peng, Xilin Feng, Lang Xiao, Tong Liu, Qilin Han, Ya-Qing Bie, Ning Kang, Xiaosong Wu, Yanfei Wu, Shouguo Wang, Kam Tuen Law, Shuo Wang, Dapeng Yu, Ben-Chuan Lin

Left arrows mark Tconset = 4.08 K and Tczero = 3.39 K....</sub>

Diode efficiency of Device 13 under magnetic fields between -0.2 and 0.2 T, with nine consecutive sweeps at each field. The field fixes the diode polarity and, at larger magnitude, stabilizes the amplitude.</sub>

**Summary.** This experimental work investigates the superconducting diode effect in CsV3Sb5, finding that the effect only appears when inversion symmetry is externally broken. Crucially, the authors link this non-reciprocal supercurrent to the presence of charge-density-wave order, demonstrating that suppressing the CDW order eliminates the diode effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed experimental test of symmetry protection in superconductivity, linking macroscopic transport phenomena (diode effect) to microscopic electronic orders (CDW) and symmetry breaking mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin and symmetry requirements for the field-free superconducting diode effect (SDE) in the Kagome superconductor CsV3Sb5.

**Main result.** The SDE requires extrinsic inversion symmetry breaking and is linked to time-reversal symmetry (TRS) breaking associated with the charge-density-wave (CDW) order, as its disappearance upon CDW suppression confirms this link.

**Method.** The authors performed transport measurements (dI/dV) on fabricated devices, systematically inducing symmetry breaking via surface oxidation or asymmetric etching, and comparing results with doped samples.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Kagome superconductor CsV3Sb5, which is centrosymmetric. The study probes the superconducting state's response to symmetry breaking, particularly focusing on the interplay between inversion symmetry, TRS, and CDW order.

**Key observables.** Diode asymmetry parameter ($\eta$), nonreciprocal supercurrent ($	ext{I}_{c+} 
eq 	ext{I}_{c-}$), and the stability of the diode response under applied magnetic fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (low T, e.g., 1.8 K), applied magnetic fields (out-of-plane and in-plane), and the degree of CDW order (controlled by Ti doping).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial pristine state is assumed to be centrosymmetric and thus diode-inactive; the stochastic nature is interpreted as arising from metastable domain configurations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the induction of SDE via oxidation/etching, show the stochastic nature of the response across multiple sweeps, demonstrate field stabilization of the diode polarity, and confirm the loss of SDE upon Ti doping.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the baseline (pristine, no SDE), then demonstrates the activation of SDE through controlled symmetry breaking (oxidation/etching). It characterizes the stochastic nature and field dependence, and finally confirms the underlying physical mechanism by showing the SDE vanishes when the CDW order is suppressed by doping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Field-free superconducting diode effects require both inversion-symmetry breaking and an internal time-reversal-symmetry (TRS) breaking field, making them sensitive probes of hidden order in superconductors. In centrosymmetric kagome AV$_3$Sb$_5$, the inversion symmetry generally should generally preclude the observation of the superconducting diode effect. Furthermore, though TRS breaking has been reported in the superconducting regime of CsV$_3$Sb$_5$, whether it is generated by superconductivity or inherited from charge-density-wave (CDW) order remains unresolved. Here we show that pristine CsV$_3$Sb$_5$ devices exhibit no intrinsic field-free superconducting diode effect, whereas surface oxidation or asymmetric etching activates a large nonreciprocal supercurrent. Moreover, the response is stochastic, with sweep-dependent polarity and magnitude, indicating metastable TRS-breaking domain configurations. Small out-of-plane magnetic fields stabilize the superconducting diode response, consistent with field selection of such domains. Finally, when long-range CDW order is suppressed by Ti doping, the SDE disappears. Our results establish the symmetry requirements for the field-free SDE in CsV$_3$Sb$_5$, reveal its stochastic domain-controlled character, and link superconducting-state TRS breaking to CDW-related order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports a major advance in solid-state quantum emitters by demonstrating precise, depth-controlled placement of bright quantum emitters in Gallium Nitride. By engineering the growth process using a low-temperature interlayer, the authors overcome the natural limitation of emitters being stuck at the substrate interface. This capability is crucial for realizing efficient, cavity-enhanced quantum light sources for quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenge of coupling solid-state quantum emitters to optical cavities, which is a cornerstone problem in quantum optics and quantum information processing. The precise depth control mechanism is highly relevant for designing integrated quantum photonic circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is the poor integration of bright, room-temperature quantum emitters (QEs) in Gallium Nitride (GaN) into functional photonic structures due to their natural location near the substrate interface.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a breakthrough by using a low-temperature GaN interlayer to precisely control the placement of QEs at arbitrary depths within the epilayer, enabling efficient coupling to optical cavities.

**Method.** The study combines advanced micro-photoluminescence ($\mu$-PL) mapping, depth-resolved spectroscopy, and epitaxial growth engineering (using low-temperature interlayers) to physically manipulate and characterize the emitters.

**Model / system.** The physical system is radiative point defects acting as single-photon emitters within GaN epilayers grown on sapphire. The methodology involves controlling the growth process (e.g., introducing a low-temperature GaN interlayer) to dictate the physical location of these defects.

**Key observables.** Narrow linewidths ($\sim 4-6$ nm), high photon count rates ($> 2$ MHz), strong antibunching ($g^{(2)}(0) \le 0.06$), and precise depth control (sub-60 nm accuracy).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, visible to telecom O-band emission, and the ability to place emitters independent of the substrate material.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optical properties of the intentionally introduced QEs are assumed to be comparable to naturally occurring ones, and the depth profiling relies on empirical matching to structural measurements (SEM/SIMS).

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\mu$-PL maps confirming emitter localization, depth-resolved XZ PL maps demonstrating depth control, and spectra confirming high-quality single-photon emission characteristics.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from identifying the initial limitation (interface confinement) to developing an engineering solution (LT interlayer deposition) and finally confirming the success of this method by showing depth-tunable, high-quality emitters compatible with cavity fabrication.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bright quantum emitters are key components for quantum communication systems. Radiative point defects in gallium nitride (GaN) are promising candidates for room-temperature single-photon emission, operating from the visible to the telecom O-band. Despite their potential, their integration into photonic structures has remained limited in the literature, with experimental photon extraction efficiencies far below simulated predictions. To identify the origin of this limitation, we investigate visible and near-infrared quantum emitters in GaN epilayers grown on $c$-plane sapphire substrates exhibiting narrow linewidths ($\sim$4 nm), high photon count rates ($ > $2 MHz), and strong antibunching, reaching $g^{(2)}(0)$ values as low as 0.06 at room temperature. We find that these emitters are located near the GaN/substrate interface, explaining their limited coupling to optical modes. Building on this observation, we show that the insertion of a thin low-temperature GaN interlayer enables the formation of quantum emitters at arbitrary depths with sub-60 nm accuracy, independent of the substrate. The intentionally introduced emitters retain optical properties comparable to naturally occurring ones, including narrow linewidths ($\sim$6 nm), saturation count rates exceeding 1.5 MHz, high Debye-Waller factors (0.69-0.98), and strong antibunching. The resulting epilayer fully coalesces within less than 250 nm ensuring compatibility with GaN-based cavity fabrication and enabling emitter placement within intrinsic regions of p-i-n diode architectures. These results mark a decisive step toward efficient emitter-cavity coupling, enabling the realization of cavity-enhanced quantum emission in GaN.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally validates a comprehensive theory describing slip pulses, which are key features of large natural earthquakes. By conducting lab experiments, the authors confirmed that the evolution of these pulses follows predicted slow, unsteady dynamics, demonstrating the theoretical attractor governing their size and peak slip rate. This provides major progress in understanding the physics of earthquake rupture.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on geophysics, the underlying mathematical framework—analyzing the stability and evolution of localized, non-linear structures (pulses) governed by rate-dependent constitutive laws—shares mathematical parallels with modeling non-equilibrium dynamics in complex quantum systems or open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the spatiotemporal dynamics of frictional rupture, specifically focusing on the behavior of 'slip pulses' which are considered a dominant rupture mode in natural earthquakes.

**Main result.** Laboratory experiments successfully confirmed the theoretical predictions regarding the intrinsically slow, unsteady evolution of pulse-like earthquakes, including the predicted transition between decaying and slowly growing pulse regimes.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling based on rate-and-state friction laws with extensive laboratory earthquake experiments using controlled fault interfaces.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a frictional interface (fault) between two elastic blocks. The dynamics are governed by a constitutive law incorporating prestresses and wave radiation, leading to the concept of self-healing slip pulses.

**Key observables.** Pulse size L(t), peak slip rate v_max(t), and the dimensionless growth rate of the pulse.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Prestress levels (sigma, tau), fault roughness amplitudes, and the time-dependent evolution parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that steady-state solutions are categorically unstable but form a dynamic attractor for the unsteady pulse evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, profiles of slip pulses, and superpositions of measured v_max vs. L data points confirming the theoretical attractor line.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework for slip pulses, detailing the experimental setup and measurement techniques (DIC), presenting the confirmation of the time-dependent dynamics, and finally demonstrating the predicted transition between pulse growth and decay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large natural earthquakes are typically mediated by frictional pulse-like rupture, which features a finite slipping zone. Recently, a comprehensive two-dimensional theory of frictional slip pulses has been developed. It predicts that pulses are categorically unstable rupture modes, whose evolution is intrinsically slow. Unsteady pulses satisfy an equation of motion expressed in terms of discrete observables, which is inherently related to their steady-state counterparts. The theory also predicts a transition from decaying to slowly growing pulses. Here, we perform extensive lab earthquake experiments to test the theory. The experiments confirm the theoretical predictions for pulse-like lab earthquakes over a range of prestress levels, rupture nucleation conditions and small-scale fault roughness amplitudes. Specifically, the predicted time-dependent dynamics are tested in a plane defined by the evolving pulse size and peak slip rate, along with measurements of the dimensionless growth rate of pulses, demonstrating their intrinsically slow unsteady nature. The predicted transition between decaying and growing pulses is also experimentally demonstrated. These results constitute major progress in understanding a dominant earthquake rupture mode.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the dynamic precession of electric polarization in a ferroelectric material to generate propagating, chirality-selective phonons in an adjacent ordinary dielectric. By coupling the polarization's intrinsic angular momentum to the lattice via electrostriction, the authors show this method is highly efficient and electrically tunable, opening a new route for programmable phononic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter/solid-state physics, the concept of injecting angular momentum into a lattice via an external, controllable field (polarization) is analogous to driving non-equilibrium quantum systems or studying energy/momentum transfer in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for an efficient and controllable method to generate propagating chiral phonons in ordinary dielectric materials, overcoming limitations associated with naturally chiral crystals.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that ferroelectric polarization precession can act as a powerful electrical source, pumping chirality-selective phonons with an efficiency significantly higher than thermal generation in common materials like LiNbO3|YAG.

**Method.** The mechanism is derived theoretically by coupling the intrinsic angular momentum of the precessing electric polarization to the lattice vibrations via electrostrictive coupling.

**Model / system.** The model considers a bilayer structure (Ferroelectric|Dielectric) and uses the elastic Lagrangian density incorporating an electrostrictive coupling term. The dynamics are solved using Green's function approaches.

**Key observables.** Chirality-selective Phonon Angular Momentum (PAM) density ($\langle l(z) angle_{dc}$), which is the primary diagnostic for the pumped chirality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Polarization amplitude ($P_0, A$), frequency ($\omega$), and the strength of the electrostrictive coupling tensor components.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of strong electrostrictive coupling and models the polarization dynamics using a monochromatic precession.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares the proposed chiral-phonon pumping in a ferroelectric|dielectric bilayer against thermal generation in chiral crystals. Figure 2 shows the spatial and frequency dependence of the induced PAM density components.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, derives the governing equations based on electrostrictive coupling, calculates the pumped angular momentum density using Green's functions, and quantifies the pumping efficiency by comparing it to thermal limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of chiral phonons has expanded the conventional view of lattice vibrations as passive heat carriers, opening new opportunities for phononic spintronic devices. However, their realization has been largely limited to chiral crystals or to specific regions of momentum space in certain achiral materials. Here, we propose a generic mechanism for generating propagating chiral phonons in an ordinary dielectric through the precession of the electric polarization in an adjacent ferroelectric. The polarization dynamics transfers its intrinsic angular momentum to the lattice via electrostrictive coupling, thereby pumping chirality-selective phonons whose handedness is dictated by that of the polarization precession. For a typical LiNbO$_{3}$$|$Y$_{3}$Al$_{5}$O$_{12}$ bilayer, we find that the pumping efficiency quantified by an interfacial convertance substantially exceeds those of thermally induced chiral-phonon generation in chiral crystals, owing to the strong electrostrictive coupling in ferroelectrics. Our work establishes ferroelectric dynamics as a versatile electrical source of chiral phonons and provides a general route toward electrically programmable chiral-phononic and spintronic functionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates an anomalous resistance peak at zero in-plane magnetic field in twisted double bilayer graphene. The authors show this peak is highly sensitive to how the material reaches its electronic state, pointing to a mechanism rooted in the domain structure of orbital magnetism. They argue that spin, coupled to valley polarization via spin-orbit interaction, is the key degree of freedom governing this exotic transport signature.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between transport measurements and the history-dependent formation of magnetic domains touches upon non-equilibrium dynamics and emergent topological phenomena, which are relevant to understanding quantum state preparation in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the microscopic origin of a sharp, metastable peak in longitudinal resistance ($R_{xx}$) observed precisely at zero in-plane magnetic field ($B_\parallel=0$) in twisted double bilayer graphene.

**Main result.** The peak's existence and magnitude are highly sensitive to the history of gate-voltage sweeps and the applied magnetic field, strongly suggesting it is governed by the domain structure of orbital magnetism, which involves spin textures.

**Method.** The study uses low-temperature transport measurements, mapping $R_{xx}$ and $R_{xy}$ as functions of gate voltages and applied in-plane magnetic fields, complemented by differential resistance measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted double bilayer graphene (t2+2) operating in a valley-polarized quarter-metal state. The physics involves moiré superlattices, orbital magnetism, and the interplay between valley, spin, and charge degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal resistance $R_{xx}(B_\parallel)$, anomalous Hall resistance $R_{xy}$, and differential resistance $	ext{dV}_{xx}/	ext{dI}$ vs. bias current $	ext{I}_{	ext{DC}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (100-300 mK), in-plane magnetic field components ($B_x, B_y$), and the gate-voltage trajectory used to prepare the electronic state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The peak is attributed to metastable magnetic domain configurations, and the underlying physics involves spin-transfer torques acting on domain walls.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $R_{xx}(B_\parallel)$ scans showing the $B_\parallel=0$ peak, demonstrating its dependence on the approach path (gate voltage trajectory) and its robustness against out-of-plane fields ($B_z$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the resistance landscape by varying gate voltages and magnetic fields, establishing the dependence of the $B_\parallel=0$ peak on the preparation history, and finally proposing a mechanism involving spin-transfer torques at domain walls.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In graphene moirés, valley polarization gives rise to orbital magnetism, manifested as an anomalous Hall effect and resulting in Barkhausen jumps in longitudinal resistance when changing domain configurations modify quasiparticle scattering. Beyond a simple picture of polarized domains, however, spin and valley textures within and between the domains are less well understood, as is the effect of these textures on transport. In the valley-polarized quarter-metal state of twisted double bilayer graphene, a sharp and metastable peak in longitudinal resistance often appears at zero in-plane magnetic field, whose microscopic origin has yet to be identified. Here, we show that this peak depends on the configuration of domains of orbital magnetism, which is itself set by the gate-voltage trajectory used to enter the ordered state and by the magnetic field --- particularly the in-plane component --- present during that trajectory. The sensitivity of the effect to in-plane magnetic field components points to spin, linked to valley polarization through spin-orbit coupling, as the key degree of freedom in both the domain formation and the resistance peak.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the Photonic Spin-Hall Effect (PSHE) as a novel, non-invasive method to detect topological phases in materials. By measuring the frequency-dependent centroid shift of reflected light, the authors show that the resulting signature uniquely reveals whether the system possesses a time-reversal-symmetry broken topological band structure. This provides a powerful optical diagnostic tool for condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of light polarization and reflection geometry (photonic setup) to probe bulk topological invariants ($\sigma_{xy}$) is a powerful, non-invasive technique relevant to quantum optics and condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find an unambiguous and non-invasive probe to detect time-reversal-symmetry broken band topological phases.

**Main result.** The frequency dependence of the photonic spin-Hall effect's centroid shift ($\Delta_c$) exhibits a unique sign structure that allows for the unambiguous distinction between topological and trivial band phases.

**Method.** The analysis relates the measurable centroid shift ($\Delta_c$) to the optical Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$) using Fresnel reflection coefficients derived from the Kubo-Greenwood formula.

**Model / system.** The study applies to various topological systems, including 2D Chern insulators (Haldane model), 3D Weyl semimetals (WSM), and Quantum Anomalous Hall (QAH) insulators.

**Key observables.** The centroid shift ($\Delta_c$), which is the intensity-weighted average of the right and left circularly polarized beam shifts, and the optical Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frequency ($\omega$), incident angle ($	heta_i$), and topological invariants like the Chern number ($C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the PSHE shift is directly proportional to the optical Hall conductivity, and that the measurement can probe the frequency dependence of this conductivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PSHE splitting ($\Delta H_\pm$) and the frequency dependence of the centroid shift ($\Delta_c$) for various phases (Chern, WSM, QAH, NI), showing distinct sign patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the PSHE as a diagnostic tool, derives the relationship between the centroid shift and optical Hall conductivity, and then applies this formalism to model Hamiltonians for Chern insulators, WSM, and QAH phases to demonstrate the unique sign signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When a plane polarized Gaussian beam of light is incident on a surface, upon reflection it splits into right and left circularly polarized beams that are spatially separated in the direction perpendicular to the plane of incidence. This is known as the photonic spin-Hall effect (PSHE). In this work, we show that the centroid shift, which is the intensity weighted average of the shifts of the right and the left circularly polarized beams directly probes the optical Hall conductivity, which carries the essential information about the topological properties of the system. We show that the centroid shift as a function of the frequency of light has a unique sign structure depending on whether the system is in a time-reversal symmetry broken band topological phase or a trivial phase. Thus, the PSHE may serve as an unambiguous and a non-invasive probe to detect time-reversal symmetry broken band topological phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that controlling grain boundary connectivity in superconducting films via deposition pressure fundamentally alters vortex matter. By comparing films with poor vs. improved GB coupling, the authors link macroscopic transport signatures (like resistive transitions) to microscopic vortex organization, providing a practical method to engineer vortex pinning for superconducting devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the methodology of using a continuous tuning parameter (sputtering pressure) to control the connectivity of weak links and observing the resulting change in collective, ordered states (vortex lattice) is analogous to tuning disorder or coupling strength in many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine how tuning the connectivity of grain boundaries (GBs) in superconducting films reorganizes the vortex matter in real space, bridging the gap between macroscopic transport and microscopic vortex structure.

**Main result.** Increasing the sputtering pressure from 5 mTorr to 7 mTorr improves GB coupling, leading to a single-step resistive transition, higher critical current density, and a more uniform, locally ordered vortex distribution.

**Method.** The researchers used reactive magnetron sputtering to create polycrystalline NbTiN films, varying the sputtering pressure to control GB coupling, and characterized the films using electrical transport measurements, magnetic force microscopy (MFM), and quantitative vortex ordering analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is polycrystalline NbTiN superconducting thin films. The key variable is the sputtering pressure, which controls the effective coupling strength of the grain boundaries, influencing the superconducting state and vortex pinning landscape.

**Key observables.** Resistive transition steps (two-step vs. single-step), critical current density ($J_c$), second magnetization peak (SMP), vortex spatial distribution (cluster-like vs. uniform), and quantitative vortex ordering metrics ($\psi_6$, bond angles).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sputtering pressure (5 mTorr vs. 7 mTorr), magnetic field ($H$), temperature ($T$), and the resulting GB coupling strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the London vortex-line approximation for calculating local forces and assumes that the measured transport properties are sensitive to the degree of GB connectivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare XRD patterns showing texture changes, R-T curves illustrating the transition steps, and MFM images contrasting the heterogeneous, cluster-like vortices in the 5 mTorr film versus the uniform, ordered vortices in the 7 mTorr film.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares two film conditions (5 mTorr vs. 7 mTorr) by analyzing transport signatures (resistive transitions, $J_c$), magnetic response (SMP), and finally, performing detailed real-space and quantitative analysis of the resulting vortex lattice structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Grain boundaries (GBs) in polycrystalline superconducting films act as a double-edged sword: they can pin vortices or degrade superconductivity through Josephson-like weak-link coupling. Here, we demonstrate that sputtering pressure tunes GB coupling in NbTiN films and visualize its consequences for vortex matter. The 5 mTorr film exhibits dispersed grain orientations and a two-step resistive transition under field, signaling intergranular weak-link behavior. In contrast, the 7 mTorr film develops a (111) texture, a single-step transition, higher critical current density, a second magnetization peak, and a δl-type pinning response consistent with improved GB coupling. Cryogenic magnetic force microscopy reveals a spatially heterogeneous, cluster-like vortex configuration in the 5 mTorr film, whereas the 7 mTorr film hosts a more uniform distribution with enhanced local order. These results establish a connection between deposition-controlled GB connectivity, macroscopic weak-link transport, and microscopic vortex organization, providing a practical route to tailor vortex pinning in polycrystalline superconducting films.

</details>
